SYNTH-0001♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Emily Taylor♔Health Catalyst♔♔♔♔♔Honestly my bigger question for HHS right now is why my supplemental coverage through BlueCross went up 22% this past renewal cycle. We're not talking about two or three payer requirements that differ slightly. The non-medical device category is especially confusing because nobody seems to agree on where liability sits if a tool assists a decision but isn't technically making one. I've had legal counsel give me two completely different answers on that in the same quarter.⏎⏎So yes I support HHS looking at reimbursement modernization but I'd really push back on the idea that clearer payment pathways alone will move the needle. But when our research team has tried to find documentation on how these tools were validated, specifically whether the training data included adequate representation of patients with darker skin tones or patients with prior reconstructive procedures, we come up mostly empty. One platform we reviewed in early 2024 had a technical document that ran to 47 pages and mentioned demographic breakdown of training data exactly once, in a footnote, without any actual numbers.⏎⏎So if HHS is asking what regulatory action would actually help: mandatory, plain-language disclosure of training dataset composition should be a baseline requirement before any clinical AI tool gets used in a reimbursable care setting. Not buried in a white paper. Something a clinician or a patient could actually find and read.⏎⏎I'll also say that the reimbursement question is complicated for surgical specialties in a way that might not be obvious. Plastic surgery already has unusual payer dynamics because of the mix of reconstructive and cosmetic procedures. If AI tools get reimbursed in ways that favor high-volume, high-revenue settings, smaller reconstructive programs serving Medicaid patients in places like rural New Mexico are going to fall even further behind on adoption. We've seen that pattern with other health IT investments and I don't see why AI would be different.⏎⏎The equity concern isn't abstract for us. SYNTH-0003♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Sandra Smith♔National Alliance for Caregiving♔♔♔♔♔My organization, the National Alliance for Caregiving, works with family caregivers across the country, and what I keep coming back to in reviewing this RFI is a question HHS doesn't seem to be asking: who is actually supposed to benefit here?⏎⏎The framing of this request treats acceleration as self-evidently good. Get AI into clinical settings faster, reduce friction, streamline adoption. But when I talk to our members — people caring for aging parents, for children with disabilities, for spouses managing complex chronic conditions — what they describe from their encounters with AI-assisted care doesn't sound like benefit to them. It sounds like faster denials. It sounds like a nurse who used to spend twenty minutes on discharge planning now spending that time correcting what the algorithm got wrong, while still being held to the same patient throughput numbers.⏎⏎I want to be direct about something that the RFI language obscures. A significant portion of what is being labeled "AI adoption in clinical care" is actually AI adoption in clinical administration, and the distinction matters enormously. The tools getting deployed fastest are prior authorization automation, clinical documentation assistants that reduce transcription labor, and workforce scheduling systems. These tools share a common financial logic: they reduce headcount or reduce the time required per worker. That is not inherently bad, but it is not the same thing as improving care, and HHS should not conflate them.⏎⏎The survey data we've seen from radiology practices is instructive here. In a 2024 review, fewer than one in four practices were giving patients any structured disclosure about AI involvement in imaging interpretation. That's not a technology problem. That's a power problem. The practices deploying these tools have no particular incentive to tell patients about them, because disclosure creates liability and slows throughput. Acceleration, absent any requirement to disclose or audit, just means more of that.⏎⏎What concerns me specifically about the RFI's reimbursement questions is the assumption embedded in them — that the barrier to adoption is financial, and that if CMS creates the right payment codes, the market will deliver appropriate tools. Maybe. But the caregivers I work with are not going to benefit from a payment model that rewards hospitals for deploying AI if there's no corresponding accountability for what that AI does to the workers managing their family members' care. In Georgia, where we've been doing focus groups with unpaid caregivers since late 2023, the consistent theme is that the humans in clinical settings feel more stretched, not less. Nurses say they're expected to supervise AI outputs on top of their existing caseloads. That's not burden reduction. It's burden transfer.⏎⏎I'm genuinely uncertain whether HHS has the right levers here or whether this is mostly a question of labor law and antitrust enforcement that falls outside this agency's authority. But at minimum, the Department should not be in the business of accelerating adoption without asking what happens to the clinical workforce when these tools are deployed primarily to reduce labor costs rather than to support clinical judgment. The workers most affected are disproportionately women and people of color — that's not a political point, it's just a description of who does bedside nursing and home health aide work in this country.⏎⏎There is also the question of what happens to family caregivers when institutions use AI to justify earlier discharges or to automate decisions about what services a patient qualifies for. I don't have a clean answer to that. But the RFI doesn't seem to be asking it either, and someone probably should be.♔♔2026-01-24T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-24T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-24T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-22T00:00:00Z♔1♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔AI in healthcare threatens to be used not to reduce clinician burnout but to justify staffing cuts, intensify surveillance, automate denials, and devalue professional expertise.♔advocacy_group♔medium♔hopeful♔Georgia♔policy director♔63♔A 2024 policy survey conducted across RBMA's 2,100 member practices found that 68% had not yet implemented formal protocols for auditing AI diagnostic tools for demographic bias, and fewer than one in four reported offering patients any structured disclosure about AI involvement in their imaging interpretations. SYNTH-0004♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Steven Lee♔♔♔♔♔♔My brother Dale spent nearly four months being bounced around by a regional clinic in Bismarck after an automated screening tool categorized him as low-risk. The staff just went with what the system said. Nobody sat down and looked at him the way a doctor actually looks at a patient. By the time someone did, his situation had gotten considerably worse than it had to be. That's where I'm coming from with this comment.⏎⏎What bothers me about this whole effort to speed up AI adoption in clinical care is that it seems to treat the problem of medicine as basically an information problem. Like if you just get the right data processed fast enough, you get good care. But that's not what I watched happen with my brother. The information about him was right there in the system. The problem was that nobody questioned the output. The staff deferred to the tool, and Dale got treated like a checkbox instead of a sick person who was scared and needed someone to actually engage with him.⏎⏎I work as a librarian. I spend a lot of time thinking about how people find and use information, and one thing I've noticed is that tools are only as good as the judgment of the people using them. We've had this debate in library science for decades, honestly. Databases don't replace the reference interview. They never have. There's something that happens when a person sits across from another person and pays attention that no search algorithm captures. I imagine medicine is the same way, probably more so.⏎⏎What I want HHS to think hard about before pushing faster adoption is the question of accountability. If an AI tool flags someone incorrectly, or misses something, who is answerable? The vendor? The clinic administrator who decided to deploy it? The nurse who was covering three patients at once and trusted the screen because she didn't have time not to? I don't think those questions are anywhere close to settled, and I worry that moving fast on adoption means moving fast past those questions rather than through them.⏎⏎Dale's situation resolved, eventually. He's doing okay now. But four months is a long time when something is wrong with you and nobody's treating it. And I keep thinking about how the efficiency argument was probably part of why that clinic adopted that tool in the first place. Smaller staff, faster throughput, lower costs. I get it. Rural healthcare is stretched thin. That's real. But I don't think the answer to being stretched thin is a system that gives staff permission to stop looking at the patient.⏎⏎I also want to say something about the rural context specifically, because I don't think it gets enough attention in these discussions. The clinics serving communities like mine in North Dakota are often understaffed to begin with, and when a new tool comes in, there's enormous pressure to use it as a substitute for the capacity you don't have rather than as a supplement to the capacity you do. That's a structural problem that no AI deployment policy fixes on its own, and I'd want HHS to be honest about that rather than suggesting that AI access is the same thing as healthcare access.⏎⏎I don't know enough about the specific regulatory mechanisms HHS is considering to comment on most of the technical questions. But on the basic question of whether we should be moving quickly or carefully on this, my answer is carefully. SYNTH-0006♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Donald Baker♔Family Caregiver Alliance♔♔♔♔♔My organization has worked with family caregivers and the clinical teams that serve them for over thirty years, and I want to be direct about something that I think is getting lost in this conversation about AI acceleration: the thing that makes clinical care work is not accurate information retrieval. It never was.⏎⏎What I keep coming back to, reading HHS's framing here, is the assumption baked into the premise. The question of how to "accelerate adoption" treats medicine as though the core problem is throughput. But I've spent years watching caregivers navigate a system that already treats their loved ones as cases rather than people, and I can tell you the erosion of human presence in clinical encounters is already doing real damage. Adding AI tools without serious guardrails is going to make that worse, not better.⏎⏎A family caregiver in a rural Vermont clinic is not presenting as a dataset. She's scared. She's been awake for 36 hours. She's watching her husband's face for signs she can't quite name. The clinician who sits across from her and reads that situation — who notices she's deflecting, who catches that she said "fine" but her hands are shaking — that clinician is doing something that has measurable clinical value. There's actually a fair body of research on therapeutic alliance and health outcomes going back to the 1990s that HHS should be looking at before they talk about replacing judgment with algorithmic recommendations.⏎⏎Our Q3 2024 policy review found that member organizations serving Medicaid-dependent and rural populations were nearly twice as likely to report inadequate vendor disclosure about model training data. Nearly twice as likely. And fewer than 12% of our 2,100 member organizations had any formal protocol for logging concerns about AI bias. So we're being asked to accelerate adoption in populations that are already getting the least accountability from vendors, in settings where the therapeutic relationship is already under the most strain from understaffing and resource shortfalls.⏎⏎I'm not categorically opposed to AI tools in clinical settings. That would be a foolish position. What I'm opposed to is the framing that treating medicine as an information-processing problem is a neutral or benign choice. When a patient becomes primarily the object of an AI recommendation pipeline, something shifts. The clinician's sense of individual moral responsibility for that specific person changes. That's not a soft concern. Distributed or attenuated responsibility in clinical decision-making has concrete consequences for how errors get caught and who gets blamed when things go wrong.⏎⏎HHS is asking specifically about what regulatory and reimbursement actions could support adoption. My answer is that before reimbursement structures reward AI-assisted clinical decisions, you need mandatory bias auditing requirements with public disclosure, you need clear liability frameworks that keep a human clinician legally and professionally responsible for every consequential recommendation, and you need some honest accounting of what we lose when the person in the exam room knows their doctor is deferring to a system rather than thinking about them.⏎⏎The therapeutic relationship is not a luxury feature that can be stripped out once the technology gets good enough. It is part of what patients are seeking when they seek care. The sense that a specific trained human being is genuinely responsible for your situation, is thinking about you rather than about your data — that matters to outcomes. It matters to whether patients disclose symptoms, follow through on treatment, trust the system at all.⏎⏎I'd also note that the RFI doesn't ask what happens to clinical training pipelines if residents and new clinicians develop their diagnostic reasoning in an environment where AI tools are doing the pattern recognition. That seems like a question worth asking before we have the infrastructure locked in.♔♔2026-01-16T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-16T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-24T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-22T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔Medicine is fundamentally a human relationship built on judgment, context, and trust — not an information-processing problem that AI can solve.♔advocacy_group♔medium♔frustrated♔Vermont♔research director♔58♔A 2024 survey of our 2,100 member organizations found that 68% reported encountering at least one instance in which an AI-assisted clinical decision tool produced recommendations that staff believed reflected demographic bias, yet fewer than 12% of those organizations had a formal protocol for logging or escalating such concerns. SYNTH-0007♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Christopher Thomas♔♔♔♔♔♔Thirty-one years on cardiac floors in Tucson and I'll tell you what actually keeps patients from getting good care, and it isn't a shortage of algorithms. It's insurance companies and their pharmacy benefit managers eating up a huge chunk of every healthcare dollar before the money ever gets anywhere near a patient or a nurse or a doctor.⏎⏎I'm not sure this RFI is the right place to say this, but I keep thinking nobody at HHS is going to say it if regular people don't. We had a patient back around 2019, diabetic woman from the Tohono O'odham Nation, and we almost missed her blockage because three different screening tools flagged her low risk. Her floor nurse pushed back, a doctor actually listened, and she went home to her grandchildren. That story is about bias in datasets and I understand that's what this RFI is partly about. But you know what else almost killed her? The four-day prior authorization delay on her follow-up medication because her PBM kept kicking back the request.⏎⏎So here's my frustration. HHS wants to talk about accelerating AI adoption in clinical care, and I get it, there's real potential there, I'm not dismissing that. But we're talking about layering more technology onto a system where pharmacy benefit managers are taking somewhere between $200 and $400 billion annually depending on which estimate you look at, and almost none of that money is generating patient care. It's generating profit for middlemen. And this document doesn't touch that. Not a word.⏎⏎I worked with nurses who spent forty-five minutes on a single prior auth call. Forty-five minutes. That's not a problem an AI diagnostic tool solves. That's a structural problem that HHS has the authority to address and keeps not addressing.⏎⏎I understand AI tools can help with some of the administrative burden and I do think there's something to that. If a good algorithm can speed up a prior auth review that's been sitting for three days, okay, I'm listening. SYNTH-0008♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Thomas Baker♔University of Oregon♔♔♔♔♔My concern is not with AI in healthcare per se. I have spent the better part of three decades doing biostatistics, most recently at the University of Oregon, and I have watched enough promising tools get chewed up by the gap between controlled validation and actual clinical deployment that I am not reflexively hostile to new technology. What I am is deeply skeptical of the framing here.⏎⏎HHS is asking how to accelerate adoption. That is the wrong question, and I want to be direct about why.⏎⏎When you train an algorithm on historical claims data, you are training it on decades of documented inequity. Insurance denial rates, diagnostic delays, differential treatment recommendations by race and insurance status — all of that gets baked in. The algorithm learns the world as it has been, not as it should be. That is not a novel observation; researchers have been publishing on this for years, including work out of Obermeyer et al. in Science back in 2019 showing a widely deployed commercial algorithm was effectively systematically underestimating the health needs of Black patients by using cost as a proxy for need. What concerns me is that the response from the policy community has been largely rhetorical acknowledgment followed by continued deployment pressure.⏎⏎The specific thing I keep coming back to is automation bias. Clinicians are, in general, less likely to override a system output that arrives with any kind of algorithmic authority attached to it. There is a reasonable body of literature on this. When a risk score or a recommendation gets surfaced by a tool the institution has purchased and the vendor has certified, it carries a different kind of weight than a colleague's opinion. That weight is not earned. It is borrowed from the general cultural prestige of quantitative methods. As a statistician I find that deeply frustrating because it means that when the model is wrong in a systematic way — when it is underperforming for elderly patients, or patients with limited English proficiency, or patients in rural Alabama where the training data was thin — those errors are less likely to get caught and corrected than random noise would be.⏎⏎The ACR apparently found, in a survey of over 1,100 radiologists, that 63% felt vendor-reported accuracy metrics did not reflect what they were seeing in practice. I do not find that surprising at all. Benchmark datasets are not patient populations. Transfer learning across hospital systems is genuinely hard. And yet we are talking about acceleration.⏎⏎What I would ask HHS to consider is whether any of its investment priorities explicitly condition funding or reimbursement eligibility on prospective bias auditing with pre-specified demographic subgroup reporting requirements. Not post-hoc. Prospective. The audit design needs to be part of the deployment plan before the system goes live, not a checkbox added two years later when someone complains. And the subgroups need to be defined in advance, because if you let vendors cherry-pick the subgroups they report on, you will get exactly the subgroups where the tool performs well.⏎⏎I recognize there is a real tension here. Some of these tools, particularly in imaging, do appear to have genuine clinical utility for certain tasks, and if the perfect is the enemy of the good, delayed adoption has its own costs. I am not dismissing that. But the asymmetry matters. If we slow down a tool that would have worked, that is a cost we can revisit. If we scale a biased tool to population level before we have the auditing infrastructure to detect what it is doing, the harm is diffuse and very hard to attribute and almost certainly will not be traced back to the deployment decision in any actionable way.⏎⏎The question of who bears the burden of proving equity should be answered before any specific reimbursement pathway gets established. Right now I am not confident HHS has that answer, or even that the question is being asked with the same urgency as the acceleration question.♔♔2026-01-15T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-15T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-24T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-22T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔AI systems in clinical settings inherit and amplify racial, socioeconomic, and institutional biases from training data, and their apparent authority makes those biases harder to detect and correct.♔academic♔medium♔frustrated♔Alabama♔biostatistician♔61♔An ACR analysis of 214 peer-reviewed clinical AI studies published between 2020 and 2023 found that fewer than 18% reported sufficient methodological detail to allow independent replication of performance benchmarks. SYNTH-0010♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Donald Brown♔Center for Democracy and Technology♔♔♔♔♔The framing of this RFI bothers me, and I want to be direct about why. HHS is asking how to "accelerate" AI adoption in clinical care, which presupposes that speed is the right goal. We don't think it is, and I want to focus my comments specifically on the questions around implementation incentives and what the Department frames as "barriers to adoption."⏎⏎The Center for Democracy and Technology works extensively on algorithmic accountability, and over the past two years we have seen a consistent pattern in how health systems are actually deploying these tools. The gap between how AI is marketed to regulators and payers and how it functions at the point of care is significant. Hospitals and insurers have every financial reason to use AI as a cost-reduction mechanism rather than a care-improvement one. Prior authorization automation is the clearest example. The tools being deployed by major commercial insurers are not primarily designed to improve clinical outcomes. They are designed to process denial decisions faster and at lower administrative cost. UnitedHealth Group's use of an algorithm called nH Predict to deny post-acute care claims, which drew substantial scrutiny in late 2023 and into 2024, is exactly the kind of deployment that this RFI's framing of AI as a care accelerant tends to obscure.⏎⏎I keep thinking about the labor dimensions of this, which the RFI doesn't really address at all. AI-powered productivity monitoring, automated clinical documentation tools, and algorithmic scheduling systems are changing what it means to work as a nurse, a medical coder, a home health aide. Many of those jobs are held disproportionately by women and workers of color. When a documentation tool adds fifteen minutes of review burden per patient encounter because the generated note keeps misattributing medications or getting the laterality wrong, that cost doesn't show up in any ROI calculation the vendor presents to the C-suite. It shows up in the body of the nurse at 10pm. The promise that these tools reduce clinician burnout is genuinely unverified at scale. I am not saying it's impossible that they help in some settings, but the evidence base is thin and the anecdotal reports coming out of implementation sites are mixed at best.⏎⏎There is also a power problem that HHS needs to take seriously. When an AI system generates a care recommendation and a frontline worker disagrees with it, what institutional mechanism exists for that worker to push back? In most settings, none that are meaningful. The vendor's system is a black box. The hospital's contract with the vendor almost certainly includes clauses that limit the hospital's ability to audit the model or share its outputs externally. The worker has no standing, often no time, and may face implicit pressure to defer to the system because overrides create documentation obligations. This is a transfer of decision-making authority to opaque third-party vendors who have no accountability relationship with patients or with communities.⏎⏎HHS should not be asking how to remove barriers to adoption. It should be asking what conditions would make adoption trustworthy. Those are different questions and they lead to different policy answers.⏎⏎Specifically regarding the RFI's questions on reimbursement incentives: we strongly oppose any approach that ties payment incentives to AI adoption rates or that treats AI integration as a quality indicator absent evidence of patient benefit. Attaching financial rewards to adoption creates exactly the wrong pressure on health systems. It will push deployment timelines faster than safety or equity review can keep pace with, and it will reward the systems with the most capital to spend on vendor contracts rather than the ones delivering the best care.⏎⏎One more thing I want to flag. The demographic disparities in how algorithmic systems affect patients are already documented well enough to act on. CDT has reviewed data from multiple sources, including survey findings presented at the National Multiple Sclerosis Society's 2024 Annual Research and Policy Summit, showing that Black and Hispanic patients with MS were significantly more likely than white patients to report having treatment plans altered by automated systems without explanation. That is not a hypothetical future harm. HHS accelerating adoption without mandatory bias audits, without patient notification requirements, and without accessible appeals processes will make those disparities worse.⏎⏎The question of who captures the financial benefit of healthcare AI and who absorbs its costs is not a technology question. It's a governance question, and right now the answer is that vendors and health system executives capture the benefit and workers and patients♔♔2026-01-24T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-24T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-24T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-22T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔AI in healthcare threatens to be used not to reduce clinician burnout but to justify staffing cuts, intensify surveillance, automate denials, and devalue professional expertise.♔advocacy_group♔medium♔urgent♔Maine♔director of government affairs♔51♔A 2023 survey of more than 4,200 people living with MS conducted by the National Multiple Sclerosis Society found that 38% of respondents from rural or low-income communities reported experiencing delays or denials in care they attributed to automated insurance or care-management systems. SYNTH-0016♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Karen Rodriguez♔Center for Responsible AI in Medicine♔♔♔♔♔The question I keep coming back to, reading through this RFI, is who exactly gets left holding the bag when an AI system gets something wrong and a patient is hurt because of it. HHS is asking how to accelerate adoption, but I haven't seen equivalent urgency around the question of legal and institutional accountability, and that gap is the thing that worries me most about the direction this process seems to be heading.⏎⏎I'll explain why this matters specifically for the practices we work with. Our center conducted a policy review of 22 commercially available radiology AI products — this was completed in early 2024 — and fewer than a third of those products published disaggregated performance metrics broken down by race, ethnicity, or socioeconomic status. That's a concrete, documentable gap. But when I try to think through what happens if one of those products performs significantly worse for, say, Black patients at a federally qualified health center in rural Minnesota, and a missed diagnosis causes harm, the accountability picture becomes genuinely incoherent. The clinician points to the tool. The hospital points to the vendor contract. The vendor contract — and I've reviewed several of these — routinely includes language disclaiming any clinical responsibility, often in pretty explicit terms, sometimes buried under FDA clearance language that implies a level of context-specific validation that the clearance process doesn't actually require.⏎⏎FDA 510(k) clearance for an AI diagnostic tool tells you something about substantial equivalence to a predicate device. It does not tell you how the system performs on the specific patient population at a specific deployment site, trained on whatever data was used, integrated into whatever EHR workflow that institution happens to run. I'm not saying FDA clearance is meaningless. But I am saying that clinicians and administrators — and patients — reasonably read "FDA cleared" as meaning "safe and effective for use," and that's not what the clearance actually certifies. The gap between those two things is where liability disappears.⏎⏎What I want HHS to specifically address, and what I think this RFI is currently underweighting, is the question of workflow lock-in. Once an AI system is embedded in clinical workflows and tied to reimbursement coding structures, it becomes practically very difficult to remove even when problems are identified. We've seen this with other health IT systems. The costs of removal, retraining, and workflow redesign create enormous institutional inertia, and what happens is that administrators end up tolerating known performance issues because the alternative is too expensive and disruptive. If HHS accelerates adoption through reimbursement incentives before accountability structures are in place, we are going to bake that dynamic in from the start.⏎⏎I want to be honest that I don't have a fully formed proposal here. I think there are genuine arguments for moving faster on some of this, and there are real patients who could benefit from AI-assisted diagnostics right now. But the accountability piece isn't a detail to work out later. Tort law as it currently stands does not map cleanly onto AI-assisted clinical decisions where the causal chain runs through a vendor's training data, a hospital's implementation choices, and a clinician's interpretation of a system output. State medical malpractice frameworks weren't designed for this. Federal preemption questions around AI device regulation are genuinely unsettled.⏎⏎The specific ask I'd make of HHS is that any reimbursement pathway for AI-assisted clinical services include, as a condition, documented vendor disclosure of training data composition and deployment-context performance metrics, and that the Department work with CMS to define what institutional responsibility looks like when an AI-assisted decision contributes to patient harm. I recognize the second part of that is genuinely complicated and probably requires coordination with DOJ and with Congress. But at minimum HHS should not be creating reimbursement incentives for technology deployment in an accountability vacuum, and I think the current draft of this RFI doesn't adequately♔♔2026-01-03T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-03T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-24T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-22T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔Embedding AI in healthcare diffuses responsibility so thoroughly that when errors occur — and they will — no single actor can be held accountable, creating a system where patients are harmed with no meaningful recourse.♔advocacy_group♔medium♔supportive♔Minnesota♔research director♔37♔A 2023 survey of 847 radiology practice administrators conducted by our association found that 61% reported having no formal protocol for auditing AI diagnostic tools for demographic bias prior to clinical deployment. SYNTH-0017♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Linda Baker♔Louisiana State University Health Sciences Center♔♔♔♔♔My concern with this RFI is not about AI in medicine per se — I have spent enough years studying health policy to know that technological change in clinical settings is neither uniformly good nor bad, and I hold no romantic attachment to the status quo. What bothers me is something more structural, and I want to try to articulate it carefully because I think it keeps getting lost in these public processes.⏎⏎The framing of this RFI treats acceleration as a self-evidently correct goal. Regulatory levers, reimbursement structures, and federal R&D investment are all being pointed in the same direction simultaneously, and the implicit argument is that the main obstacles to AI adoption are bureaucratic friction and insufficient funding. But that framing skips over a question that I would argue belongs much earlier in the process: how much computational delegation of clinical judgment do we, as a society, actually want? That is not a technical question. It is a political and moral one, and an administrative agency issuing an RFI is not the right mechanism for answering it.⏎⏎Here is what I keep thinking about. Once a technology is embedded in reimbursement schedules, it becomes practically irreversible. CMS coding decisions, once made, take years to revisit and often never fully unwind. If HHS uses payment policy to drive AI adoption into clinical workflows at scale — and this RFI explicitly contemplates doing exactly that — the practical consequence is that future policymakers, future clinicians, and future patients inherit a set of defaults they had no meaningful role in choosing. The democratic window for deciding how much AI belongs in the exam room closes quietly and without a vote.⏎⏎The radiology context is actually a good illustration of what I mean, though I recognize it is only one corner of the problem. A 2024 ACR Data Science Institute survey found that 68% of radiology practices had deployed at least one AI-enabled imaging tool, but fewer than 12% had done any systematic internal validation to check whether vendor performance claims held up under their own patient populations. That is a striking gap, and it exists already, before any federal acceleration agenda has been fully operationalized. What happens to that gap when reimbursement incentives are added on top?⏎⏎I want to be fair to HHS here. I understand that there are genuine access and efficiency problems that AI tools might help address, particularly in underserved rural areas like much of Idaho, where I spent considerable time before my current position. I am not arguing for a moratorium. What I am arguing is that the sequencing is wrong. Governance frameworks, audit requirements, and some form of democratic deliberation about acceptable tradeoffs should precede the use of federal payment policy as an adoption accelerant, not follow it.⏎⏎The RFI asks specifically about regulatory actions HHS can take to accelerate adoption. My answer to that particular question is that HHS should first commission an independent analysis of how reversible its proposed policy interventions actually are — not just legally, but practically, given how institutional workflows and procurement contracts function in real hospital systems. The question of what can be undone if the evidence later turns unfavorable should be answered before the question of how to move faster.⏎⏎I realize that is probably not the kind of answer the RFI is designed to receive, but I think it is the honest one.⏎⏎One other thing that I did not see adequately addressed anywhere in the RFI document: the question of who bears liability when an AI-assisted clinical decision causes harm, and whether acceleration of adoption changes that calculus. SYNTH-0018♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Charles Scott♔Insilico Medicine♔♔♔♔♔My organization has been deploying AI-assisted documentation and triage tools across a network of 14 community clinic sites in Maryland, and what we have seen in practice is pretty far removed from how these products are described in vendor demos and pilot results. The performance gaps are real and they are not small. We had one documentation tool that cleared internal benchmarks and performed well during the initial deployment at our Rockville site, but when we rolled it across the remaining 13 locations over Q3 2023, the output quality degraded noticeably at sites where our EHR configuration differed even slightly from the reference setup the vendor used during testing. Same vendor. Same contract. Meaningfully different results.⏎⏎I keep coming back to this because the RFI frames acceleration as the primary goal, and I understand why — the potential benefits are real and I am not dismissing them. But acceleration is exactly what got health IT into the mess it has been in for the past fifteen years. EHR implementations that generated so much alert fatigue that clinicians started ignoring warnings wholesale. Sepsis prediction tools that failed quietly in community hospital settings where the training data did not reflect the patient population. These were not edge cases or isolated failures. They were the predictable result of deploying systems that performed well in controlled or academic settings into environments that were operationally messier and more variable than the developers anticipated.⏎⏎What HHS should be asking before it reduces regulatory friction is whether there is adequate infrastructure to detect performance degradation after deployment, not just before. Right now there is not, at least not at the scale of community clinic networks like ours. We do not have a clinical informatics team. We have two people who split IT responsibilities across 14 sites. If an AI triage tool starts producing worse recommendations for patients with limited English proficiency or patients on atypical medication regimens, we are probably not going to catch that systematically. We might catch it because a physician flags something, or because a patient outcome prompts a chart review. That is not a monitoring system, that is luck.⏎⏎I also want to raise something about the regulatory classification question specifically, because it affects us directly. 68% of our staff reported in an internal review last year that they were uncertain about liability exposure when using AI tools that do not fall under formal medical device classifications. That is not a compliance training problem. That is a structural ambiguity problem. If HHS accelerates adoption without resolving where liability sits for AI tools that influence clinical decisions but are not technically classified as medical devices, we are going to see administrators make one of two choices: avoid the tools entirely, or use them without any formal risk assessment. Neither of those is a good outcome.⏎⏎The 320 staff hours we spend annually reconciling inconsistent payer requirements around health technology tools — that number came from our Q1 2024 budget analysis and it was not a theoretical estimate, it was based on logged hours — that overhead is a real problem and I would welcome rationalization of those standards. But streamlining administrative requirements is a different thing from accelerating deployment timelines before we have post-market surveillance infrastructure that actually works in resource-limited settings.⏎⏎I do not have a clean answer for how to balance these things. What I can say is that the framing of this RFI makes me nervous, because it reads as though the bottleneck is regulatory friction when the bottleneck we experience operationally is reliability and interpretability after the tool leaves the controlled environment it was built in.♔♔2026-01-14T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-14T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-24T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-22T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔AI tools that appear impressive in demonstrations are often brittle in real clinical environments, adding hidden risk while creating false reassurance about safety and effectiveness.♔industry♔medium♔concerned♔Maryland♔healthcare administrator♔39♔A 2023 operational review conducted across our network of 14 community clinic sites found that 68% of staff reported uncertainty about liability exposure when using AI-assisted documentation and triage tools that fall outside formal medical device classifications. SYNTH-0019♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Sarah Martinez♔♔♔♔♔♔My first reaction when I found this notice was honestly just confusion. It also had no way of knowing that she tends to minimize what she's feeling when she thinks something official is watching or recording her. I knew those things about her because I have sat with her three mornings a week and learned how she actually is. The algorithm did not have that. She ended up in the hospital anyway and her daughter was beside herself.⏎⏎I'm not saying AI has no place in care. I genuinely don't know enough about how these systems are built to have a strong opinion on the technical side. The monitoring system output gets reviewed. SYNTH-0020♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Melissa Martinez♔Gladstone Institutes♔♔♔♔♔My work sits at the intersection of health policy research and implementation science, and I'll be honest: when I read through the framing of this RFI, my first reaction was something between recognition and dread. We have been here before. Not metaphorically — literally. The promise of health IT transforming care delivery was the defining policy conversation of 2008 through 2014, and I spent a significant portion of my career watching institutions race to install EHR systems that met certification criteria while demonstrably failing on the ground. Clinicians developed workarounds. Alert fatigue became so normalized that meaningful warnings disappeared into the noise. And the research literature documenting harm from poor implementation consistently lagged years behind deployment decisions.⏎⏎I want to focus my comments on one question the RFI raises about evidence standards, because I think it is where the structural problem is most visible and most likely to be glossed over in the enthusiasm to accelerate.⏎⏎The CAP Technology Assessment Committee evaluated 14 commercially deployed pathology AI systems between 2021 and 2023. Fewer than one-third of those evaluations included post-deployment performance data beyond the original training cohort. One-third. These are not experimental tools — they were commercially deployed, meaning clinicians were using them on real patients in real institutions. And the evidence base supporting those tools was, in the majority of cases, benchmark performance from curated datasets that may bear little resemblance to the patient populations being served.⏎⏎This is not a new finding and it is not a data collection problem that better registries alone will fix. The degradation of AI performance across hospital systems, patient populations, and EHR configurations is structural. It happens because training data encodes the practice patterns, equipment calibrations, documentation habits, and demographic distributions of the institutions where it was collected. When you deploy in a different context — say, a rural South Dakota critical access hospital versus the academic medical center where the model was validated — you are not just applying a tool, you are testing an untested hypothesis. And the hypothesis often fails quietly, which is the worst kind of failure in a clinical setting.⏎⏎What concerns me about the current RFI framing is the assumption that reimbursement incentives and regulatory streamlining are the primary levers that matter. There is very little in the solicitation that suggests HHS is grappling seriously with the automation bias problem, which is well-documented in decision science and increasingly visible in clinical settings where providers over-rely on algorithmic outputs even when their own clinical judgment would have caught an error. The sepsis prediction algorithm deployed by several major health systems — there was substantial reporting on the Epic Sepsis Model's real-world performance by 2021, published in JAMA Internal Medicine — is the case I keep returning to. High sensitivity claims in controlled evaluation, meaningful false positive rates in practice, and by the time the peer-reviewed critique was widely circulated, the tool was already embedded in workflows across hundreds of hospitals.⏎⏎Acceleration, in that context, didn't help. It compounded the exposure before the evidence caught up.⏎⏎I recognize there are genuine arguments for moving faster — clinician shortages, diagnostic access disparities in rural areas, the real potential of imaging AI in settings where specialist coverage is thin. I don't think the answer is to halt development. But I do think HHS needs to be honest about what "accelerating adoption" actually means when the evidence infrastructure is this thin. Reimbursement signals without mandatory post-market surveillance requirements are just subsidizing deployment. And if the lesson of the EHR era is that we will fund rollout but not the hard work of monitoring what happens afterward, I'm not sure what leads anyone to expect a different outcome here.⏎⏎One specific ask: HHS should require, as a condition of any preferential reimbursement treatment for AI-assisted clinical tools, that developers make site-specific validation data publicly available in a standardized format within 24 months of initial deployment. Not aggregated across sites. Per site, or at minimum stratified by institution type and patient population characteristics. Developers will push back on this as proprietary. That's a negotiation worth having openly rather than♔♔2025-12-28T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-28T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-24T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-22T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔AI tools that appear impressive in demonstrations are often brittle in real clinical environments, adding hidden risk while creating false reassurance about safety and effectiveness.♔academic♔medium♔hopeful♔South Dakota♔professor of health policy♔47♔A 2024 CAP member survey of 1,870 practicing pathologists and laboratory directors found that 58% had encountered AI-assisted diagnostic tools in clinical or research settings, yet only 23% reported having access to peer-reviewed validation studies reflecting real-world performance in their specific patient populations. SYNTH-0021♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Linda Allen♔Anxiety and Depression Association of America♔♔♔♔♔The patients we hear from at the Anxiety and Depression Association of America are not asking for faster diagnostics. But I keep coming back to it because the framing of this RFI — accelerating adoption, streamlining pathways, reducing friction — treats the problem of AI in clinical care as essentially a logistics problem. Get the tools into clinics faster. Figure out the reimbursement codes. And I think that framing is missing something that our members, both patients and clinicians, talk about constantly.⏎⏎Mental health care in particular is a relationship before it is anything else. I have been at this organization for over a decade and I have sat through more focus groups and listening sessions than I can count, and the thing people describe when they talk about a provider who actually helped them is almost never about accuracy of diagnosis. It is about feeling like another human being was truly present with them. Could read when they were deflecting. Knew when to push and when to back off. That is not a data problem. No training dataset captures what a good therapist picks up in the first five minutes of a session.⏎⏎What concerns me specifically about accelerating AI adoption in clinical settings — and this is where I want to focus because I think HHS is actually asking about implementation barriers, not just cheerleading for the technology — is that there is a kind of false objectivity that comes with algorithmic output. A physician who gets an AI-generated recommendation handed to them is in a weird position. The recommendation looks authoritative. It came from a system. And the research on automation bias is pretty clear that clinicians, under time pressure, tend to defer to that output even when their own clinical judgment is telling them something different.⏎⏎The Texas Medical Association ran a survey of over 4,200 physicians in 2024 and found that 63% were concerned that AI diagnostic tools had produced or could produce inequitable recommendations for patients in rural or low-income communities. And 41% of respondents said their patients had never been formally told that AI played a role in their assessment. That is not a minor implementation detail. That is a consent problem. And in mental health contexts specifically, where the relationship between provider and patient is doing a lot of the clinical work, having that relationship quietly mediated by an algorithm the patient doesn't know about feels like a real breach.⏎⏎I am not opposed to AI tools in healthcare categorically. There are probably administrative applications that make sense and free up clinician time. But the RFI keeps talking about clinical care, about diagnostic support, about treatment recommendations. That is a different thing entirely. When we are talking about a patient who is sitting in an office scared and not sure they want to be there, the presence of that clinician — the fact that a specific human being with their own judgment and accountability is looking at that person — is not incidental to the care. It is the care.⏎⏎HHS should at minimum require that any AI-assisted clinical assessment come with patient notification before the encounter, not buried in intake paperwork. And there should be a straightforward mechanism for patients to ask how a recommendation was generated. I am not sure what the right regulatory vehicle for that is, and I understand there are competing considerations around workflow and cost, but the current baseline of essentially no requirement is not acceptable.⏎⏎The other thing I would flag is that the research base on AI in behavioral health specifically is thin. Really thin. Most of what is cited in conversations like this comes from radiology or pathology, where the task is more bounded. Depression screening, risk assessment for self-harm, medication management for anxiety disorders — these are messier clinical problems and the evidence that AI improves outcomes in these areas is not there yet. I would like to see HHS require that any accelerated adoption pathway for AI in behavioral health settings be tied to prospective outcome data collection, not just post-market surveillance that may or may not be acted on.⏎⏎We can submit more detailed comments if HHS publishes a more targeted RFI on behavioral health applications specifically. SYNTH-0027♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Kimberly Garcia♔Arterys♔♔♔♔♔My title is Chief Medical Officer, but what I spend most of my time doing lately is explaining to payers why a tool that received FDA clearance in March of 2023 still hasn't made it onto a single coverage determination list in our region. They're not being obstructionist — they're waiting for accountability structures that don't yet exist, and no amount of CMS coding reform or accelerated clearance pathways is going to change that calculus until the underlying liability question gets answered.⏎⏎What concerns me about the framing of this RFI is that it treats adoption speed as the metric we should be optimizing for. I understand the argument. There are tools that genuinely help clinicians and patients, and yes, the current system slows them down. I believe that. But the tools that get accelerated under a streamlined regime won't only be the good ones. There's no selection mechanism being proposed here that would distinguish between a well-validated AI system with a coherent governance structure and a mediocre one with good marketing. The reimbursement lever, if pulled too fast, applies to both.⏎⏎The deeper issue — and I keep coming back to this even when I try to think about it from a different angle — is that embedding AI into reimbursement structures at scale is not really reversible. Once a CPT code exists for an AI-assisted diagnostic service, once health systems have restructured workflows around it, once you have a generation of residents who trained with these tools as standard of care, you've made a choice about how much of clinical medicine gets mediated by algorithmic systems. That choice deserves a real democratic process, not an RFI comment period.⏎⏎I want to be clear that I'm not opposed to AI in clinical care. That would be an odd position for someone in my role. But there's a version of "acceleration" that actually serves patients and clinicians, and it starts with getting the governance foundations built first — clear liability allocation for AI tools that operate outside the traditional medical device framework, coverage determination standards that don't require each regional payer to reinvent the wheel, and some kind of prospective process for deciding which clinical decisions should remain with human physicians regardless of what the model can do.⏎⏎The comment period for this RFI closes in a matter of weeks, and the questions being posed are genuinely hard ones that health systems, ethicists, patient advocates, and policymakers have been wrestling with for years without resolution. I'd ask HHS to consider whether the pace of this process itself is telling us something about the gap between where governance currently is and where it would need to be before acceleration is the right word for what we're doing.⏎⏎Regarding the specific question about regulatory and reimbursement actions HHS might take: the most useful single step, in my view, would be to convene a formal rulemaking process specifically on AI liability allocation rather than treating it as a downstream consequence of reimbursement reform. SYNTH-0029♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Kevin Flores♔National LGBTQ Task Force♔♔♔♔♔The framing of this RFI has bothered me since I first read it. HHS is asking how to accelerate AI adoption in clinical care, and I understand the appeal of that question — there are genuinely promising tools out there — but it skips over something we at the National LGBTQ Task Force have been watching with real alarm across the past two years of our health equity work. Fewer than one in five had any formal protocol for even telling patients an algorithm was involved before a treatment decision was made. That is not a technology-readiness problem. That is a rights problem. And it is happening right now, before any of the "acceleration" HHS is contemplating.⏎⏎When I try to trace what happens to a patient harmed by one of these tools, the chain of responsibility just dissolves. The clinician says they relied on the system. The hospital says the vendor is responsible. The vendor's contract almost certainly has language disclaiming liability for clinical outcomes — this is standard practice and I have seen enough of these contracts to be confident about that. And FDA 510(k) clearance, which is what most of these tools get, does not actually establish real-world safety in diverse populations. It provides a kind of regulatory paperwork that gets read as a safety endorsement even though that is not what the clearance process is designed to do.⏎⏎For our communities — LGBTQ people, particularly transgender patients and patients of color who already have documented reasons to distrust clinical institutions — this matters in a specific way. If an AI tool systematically produces worse recommendations for your demographic group because it was trained on non-representative data, and you are harmed, and then you try to figure out who you can hold responsible, the answer under current frameworks is essentially no one. The data provider didn't treat you. The vendor didn't treat you. The clinician relied on a cleared tool in good faith. The hospital followed its protocol. Existing tort law was not built for this and I am not aware of any federal regulatory framework that fills the gap.⏎⏎What I am asking HHS to do before any further acceleration is to answer, with specificity, the liability question. Not in a white paper. In binding guidance or rulemaking. Who is the responsible party when an AI-assisted clinical decision causes harm? What is the patient's recourse? The current architecture of these deployments — vendor disclaimers, opaque training data, embedding in clinical workflows so deeply that clinicians can't easily override or even identify when the AI is influencing a recommendation — is being built out at scale right now, and once it is embedded it will be practically impossible to reverse. We have seen this with EHR systems. Once a hospital has organized its entire workflow around a platform, it does not matter how many problems emerge, they are not switching.⏎⏎I recognize HHS cannot resolve tort law through an RFI response. But it can condition any new reimbursement pathways, certification programs, or federal funding tied to AI adoption on demonstrated accountability structures. If a vendor wants the benefit of federal support or recognition, they should have to agree to something meaningful when their tool hurts someone.⏎⏎The survey data I mentioned found that practices serving higher proportions of Medicaid patients were significantly less likely to have done any equity-focused validation of the tools they were already using. That means the populations with the least institutional power to push back are the ones most likely to be harmed without any recourse. I'm not sure HHS fully appreciates how fast this is moving on the ground, and how little of the infrastructure that would need to exist to make it safe actually exists yet. The practices using these tools are not waiting for federal guidance. SYNTH-0032♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Ashley Jones♔Asian Health Coalition♔♔♔♔♔My organization works with Asian American, Native Hawaiian, and Pacific Islander communities across the Midwest, and what I keep coming back to is a question that this RFI doesn't seem to ask directly: when an AI system causes a patient harm, who actually pays for that?⏎⏎I don't mean this rhetorically. I mean it as a concrete, practical question that HHS should be able to answer before it does anything to speed up adoption. Right now, the honest answer is: probably nobody.⏎⏎Here's what I've observed working in this space. A vendor sells a diagnostic support tool to a hospital system. The contract, almost certainly, contains language disclaiming liability for clinical decisions made with the tool's output. The clinician who acted on the tool's recommendation points to the fact that they were using an FDA-cleared product and following institutional protocol. The hospital says it implemented the system according to vendor specifications. The vendor says the training data was the user's responsibility to validate for their patient population, or that the clinician deviated from recommended use. You end up with injured patients who have spent months trying to figure out who to even send a demand letter to. This is not hypothetical. We are already seeing this pattern in radiology and in sepsis prediction tools.⏎⏎The FDA clearance issue genuinely frustrates me because I think it creates a false floor. A 510(k) clearance tells you that a device is substantially equivalent to something already on the market. It does not tell you that the tool performs equitably across AAPI subpopulations, or performs well at a specific community health center in Sioux Falls, or remains accurate as a hospital's patient demographics shift over time. But once a system has FDA clearance, hospitals treat it like a warranty, and vendors use it as a liability shield. The clearance becomes a way of saying "we did the right thing" without actually demonstrating that the thing works for the people it's being used on.⏎⏎There's a related problem with lock-in that I don't think gets discussed enough. When an AI tool gets embedded into clinical workflows and especially into billing and reimbursement codes, the cost of removing it becomes enormous even if problems emerge. Institutions become structurally dependent. I sat in on a session at a health equity conference in Chicago in March 2024 where a hospital administrator described being unable to sunset a deterioration prediction algorithm that their own quality team had flagged as performing worse for patients with limited English proficiency, because the system had been integrated into their EHR in a way that would cost an estimated $2.4 million to unwind. That administrator was not happy about this. But what choice did they have?⏎⏎So when HHS asks what regulatory actions it can take to accelerate adoption, I want to flag that the acceleration is already happening faster than the accountability infrastructure can handle. Before HHS does more to speed things up, I think it needs to answer who bears responsibility when these systems fail, and that answer needs to be clear enough that a patient's attorney could actually use it. Right now it isn't.⏎⏎I'm genuinely uncertain whether the right mechanism here is through CMS conditions of participation, through FTC vendor disclosure requirements, or through some kind of mandatory post-market surveillance framework tied to reimbursement. SYNTH-0035♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Richard Jones♔American Physical Therapy Association♔♔♔♔♔The question HHS is really asking here — how do we accelerate AI adoption in clinical care — starts from an assumption I think deserves a lot more scrutiny before this agency commits to a direction. Acceleration implies we know where we're going is safe. I'm not sure we do.⏎⏎My work at APTA keeps me focused mostly on physical therapy practice settings, but a significant share of the patients our members see overlap with the populations that keep showing up in the equity literature as the ones most harmed when algorithmic tools go wrong. Rural Vermonters. Medicaid patients. People who don't speak English as a first language. These are not abstract categories to our members; they're the people in the waiting room on a Tuesday morning.⏎⏎What concerns me about the framing of this RFI is that it treats equity safeguards as something to be layered on after the adoption infrastructure is built. The pulse oximetry situation is the clearest example I know of how that logic fails. Devices that systematically read oxygen saturation as normal in patients with darker skin tones were in clinical use for decades before the measurement bias was formally documented in a major peer-reviewed study (Sjoding et al., NEJM, December 2020). Decades. And that was a relatively simple sensor producing a single number. Now we're talking about deploying opaque predictive models trained on historical EHR data — data that already encodes decades of differential access to care, differential diagnosis rates, differential treatment decisions — and the proposal is essentially to move fast and figure out the equity piece later.⏎⏎I keep coming back to the prior authorization context specifically. There's been reporting, and CMS has noted it in related rulemaking contexts, that AI-assisted prior auth tools used by some commercial payers have produced denial rates that vary by patient race and income in ways that the deploying organizations have not been able to fully explain. When you ask the vendor how the model works, you often get something about proprietary methodology. That's the part that worries me most. Not that bias exists — bias in healthcare has always existed — but that the computational wrapping makes it much harder to see and therefore much harder for a clinician or a patient to push back against. A human reviewer making a bad call is at least a human you can challenge. An algorithm producing a denial looks like a fact.⏎⏎APTA's position, and my own view as someone who has spent the better part of a decade working on health policy, is that HHS should condition any accelerated adoption pathway on prospective demographic performance disclosure requirements. Not retrospective audits after harm is documented. Prospective. Meaning: before a tool is deployed in a clinical or administrative context, the developer should be required to demonstrate validation data stratified by race, ethnicity, age, primary language, and insurance status, and that data should be publicly accessible in a structured format — not buried in a 400-page 510(k) submission that most clinicians will never read.⏎⏎I'd also push back gently on the implicit assumption in this RFI that the primary barrier to AI adoption is regulatory friction. In my experience talking to practitioners — including in rural Vermont communities where some of our members run small practices — the hesitation is less about regulatory burden and more about not knowing whether these tools have been tested on patients who look like theirs. That's a trust problem, and you don't solve a trust problem by removing oversight. You solve it by building systems where the evidence of safety and fairness is actually visible to the people using the tools.⏎⏎One specific ask: the RFI asks about research and development priorities. HHS should fund — directly, through AHRQ or a designated mechanism — independent third-party audits of already-deployed clinical AI tools, with particular attention to tools used in prior authorization and diagnostic support in federally qualified health centers and rural health clinics. Not manufacturer-sponsored validation. Independent. The ADA Health Policy Institute survey data from 2023 showing that 62% of practitioners serving majority-minority populations expressed concern about demographic validation gaps suggests this is not a niche worry.⏎⏎I'll also note, and I recognize this is slightly outside the scope of the RFI as written, that the informed consent question here is genuinely unsettled in ways that HHS hasn't fully addressed. If a treatment recommendation is influenced by an AI tool, does the patient have a right to know that? What does meaningful disclosure even look like when the clinician themselves may not fully understand how the model weighted the inputs?♔♔2025-12-27T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-27T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-24T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-22T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔AI systems in clinical settings inherit and amplify racial, socioeconomic, and institutional biases from training data, and their apparent authority makes those biases harder to detect and correct.♔advocacy_group♔medium♔hopeful♔Vermont♔senior policy analyst♔38♔A 2023 ADA Health Policy Institute survey of 4,800 dental practitioners found that 38% reported serving patient populations in which more than half of individuals identified as members of racial or ethnic minority groups, low-income households, or rural communities with limited digital literacy. SYNTH-0036♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Michael Williams♔Premier Inc.♔♔♔♔♔Thirty years in practice management will make you skeptical of any process that starts with "let's move fast." I've watched three separate EHR transitions gut our clinical staff's time and morale, and in every case the pitch was the same: adopt now, work out the problems later. The problems did not work themselves out.⏎⏎So when I read through this RFI and see HHS asking how to accelerate AI adoption in clinical care, my first reaction is honestly not excitement. It's the same low-grade dread I felt in 2014 when our practice was pushed into Meaningful Use Stage 2 before anyone had figured out what the interoperability standards were actually going to look like in practice. We spent roughly $80,000 that year on compliance consultants who were, to be fair, also guessing.⏎⏎The question I want to address is the one about barriers to adoption, because I think HHS has it backward. The framing assumes that regulation is slowing down something that should go faster, but our own network data tells a different story. When Premier surveyed health technology partners in 2024, 54% of mid-sized firms said unresolved liability questions were a significant deterrent to launching new products. That's not regulators being too cautious. That's industry itself saying the foundation isn't stable enough to build on. If the companies building these tools are worried about liability exposure for AI that doesn't qualify as a medical device, then accelerating adoption without resolving that question doesn't help them either. It just transfers the risk downstream to the practices and hospitals implementing the tools, and ultimately to patients.⏎⏎There's also something that bothers me about the irreversibility of what's being considered here. Once AI documentation tools are embedded in reimbursement structures and Medicare billing workflows, removing them becomes practically impossible regardless of how they perform. That's not hypothetical, that's just how federal payment policy works. CMS added something, it becomes load-bearing infrastructure within about 18 months, and then the political cost of removing it exceeds the practical cost of keeping it even when it isn't working well. I'm not opposed to AI in clinical settings in principle. But HHS is being asked to make a one-way decision without audit requirements, without a clear governance structure for when these systems fail, and without any real public deliberation about whether faster and more automated is actually what patients want from medicine.⏎⏎I work with clinicians every day who are exhausted and who genuinely do not know whether they're supposed to be adopting these tools or waiting for guidance. The ambiguity is the problem. Not too much regulation, not too little, just the absence of anything stable to stand on. A practice manager at a 12-physician group in western Massachusetts does not have the capacity to independently evaluate AI vendor liability claims, audit model drift in a documentation tool, or negotiate meaningful indemnification language into a contract with a company that has 200 employees and a Series B. We need HHS to build the framework before it builds the on-ramp.⏎⏎And I'll be honest, I'm not even sure what the right framework looks like. But I know that "figure it out after adoption" has a track record in this sector, and it is not a good one. At minimum, before reimbursement pathways are opened up, there should be clear answers to who bears liability when an AI-assisted clinical note contains an error that contributes to a bad outcome, and what the audit rights are for practices that want to verify what these systems are actually doing. Those questions have been floating around since at least 2022 and I have yet to see anything close to a settled answer from HHS or FDA or anyone else.⏎⏎The other thing I keep coming back to is that this is genuinely a values question, not just a technical one. Whether medicine should be faster and more automated or slower and more accountable is not a question that an RFI process is equipped to resolve, and I'm not sure it should be resolved by an administrative agency on a timeline driven by where the investment money is going right now.⏎⏎I'd request that HHS at minimum publish a summary of what liability and governance questions it considers unresolved before issuing any final guidance that would accelerate reimbursement integration, so that practices like ours have some basis for understanding what we'd actually be agreeing to.♔♔2026-01-20T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-20T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-24T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-22T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔Society has the right to decide that some domains should remain stubbornly human, and once AI is embedded in healthcare it becomes practically and politically impossible to remove — making the decision to accelerate now a decision that forecloses future democratic choice.♔industry♔medium♔angry♔Massachusetts♔practice manager♔59♔A 2024 internal policy review conducted across Writerslogic's network of 47 health technology partners found that 68% identified inconsistent payer standards as the primary barrier to scaling AI-assisted documentation tools, with compliance overhead averaging $112,000 per product per payer relationship. SYNTH-0037♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Robert Lee♔American College of Preventive Medicine♔♔♔♔♔The American College of Preventive Medicine appreciates the opportunity to respond to this RFI, but I want to be direct: the framing of this solicitation concerns me before we get to any specific question. Asking how HHS can "accelerate" AI adoption in clinical and administrative settings, without first establishing what minimum safeguards must be in place before that acceleration happens, puts the cart well ahead of the horse in ways our member organizations are already experiencing on the ground.⏎⏎I want to focus my comments on the questions related to reimbursement and coverage determination functions, because that is where our members are reporting the most concentrated and immediate harm.⏎⏎ACPM Comments | HHS AI RFI | Submitted February 2025 | Page 1⏎⏎Our 2024 member survey — conducted across approximately 2,800 member organizations — found that 63% had received patient complaints specifically tied to algorithmic-driven care recommendations or coverage decisions.
