But what stands out more to me, having reviewed the underlying data from our Health Equity Working Group, is where those complaints are concentrated: facilities serving Medicaid-enrolled populations, patients with limited English proficiency, elderly patients with complex comorbidities. These are not people with robust capacity to navigate multi-step appeals processes. They are often already overwhelmed by their medical situations, and an automated denial letter — with little explanation and a 60-day appeals window that assumes they have time, language skills, and persistence — is functionally a final decision for many of them.⏎⏎The specific situation I keep returning to when I read this RFI is what happened with UnitedHealth Group's nH Predict tool, which was the subject of a class action lawsuit filed in late 2023. The allegation, supported by internal data the plaintiffs obtained, was that the algorithm was overriding treating physicians' recommendations for post-acute care at a rate that produced denials in approximately 90% of reviewed cases for certain Medicare Advantage patients. A federal court allowed that case to proceed. I'm not saying every AI tool in prior authorization functions behaves this way, but the fact that we are discussing acceleration of AI adoption in coverage functions without any serious rulemaking response to that documented episode is genuinely hard to understand from a patient protection standpoint.⏎⏎ACPM Comments | HHS AI RFI | Submitted February 2025 | Page 2⏎⏎HHS has authority here. The existing statutory framework under the Medicare Advantage regulations, the ACA's coverage determination provisions, and the ADA's requirements around meaningful program access give the Department real tools it has not fully deployed. Requiring that any AI-assisted prior authorization or coverage determination system be explainable in plain language to the patient, that a qualified human reviewer must affirmatively approve any denial (not just be nominally "available"), and that appeal timelines account for the actual burden on non-English-speaking patients — none of that requires new legislation. It requires HHS to treat acceleration and accountability as inseparable, rather than sequencing accountability as something to figure out later.⏎⏎What particularly frustrates me is that fewer than one in four of our member organizations currently provides patients with any meaningful disclosure about when AI is influencing their care or coverage decisions. Twenty-three percent. We asked that question specifically because we suspected the number would be low, and it was still worse than we expected. You cannot have an informed consent framework, you cannot have meaningful appeals rights, you cannot have health equity in AI deployment if patients don't even know an algorithm was involved in the decision that affected them.⏎⏎I recognize there are legitimate arguments for moving quickly on AI in clinical settings — diagnostic support, documentation burden reduction, population health screening. I'm not opposed to AI in medicine categorically. But the prior authorization and coverage determination context is different, and treating it the same as, say, an ambient documentation tool or a radiology assist function doesn't hold up analytically. The stakes of a wrong output are asymmetric in a very specific way: the patient bears all of the downside, and the insurer bears essentially none under the current structure.⏎⏎ACPM Comments | HHS AI RFI | Submitted February 2025 | Page 3⏎⏎The RFI asks about barriers to adoption and what HHS can do to reduce them. I'd ask the Department to also consider what barriers currently exist to patients challenging AI-driven decisions, because those barriers are structural and they are growing as these systems scale. The appeals processes most plans use were designed around human reviewer error rates and response times. They have not been updated to account for the volume of denials that an automated system can generate, or the fact that many of those denials are effectively identical because they come from the same algorithmic output applied thousands of times. A patient in Worcester, Massachusetts filing an appeal in January 2025 is essentially arguing against a policy embedded in code, with no access to the model, no explanation of which inputs drove the denial, and no meaningful way to know whether their appeal is being reviewed by someone who has actually looked at their case or by♔♔2026-02-15T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-15T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-24T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-22T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔AI-driven insurance decisions and prior authorization systems automate the denial of care in ways that are opaque, difficult to appeal, and disproportionately harmful to vulnerable patients.♔advocacy_group♔medium♔supportive♔Massachusetts♔senior policy analyst♔43♔A 2024 survey conducted across our 2,800 member organizations found that 63% reported receiving patient complaints related to algorithmic-driven care recommendations, with complaints disproportionately originating from facilities serving Medicaid-enrolled and non-English-speaking populations. SYNTH-0039♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Betty Anderson♔National Association of Community Health Centers♔♔♔♔♔Our network serves patients across Mississippi — rural counties, Delta communities, places where the nearest specialist is two hours away and Medicaid is the only coverage most families have ever known. Because right now, the honest answer from our affiliated clinics is that there basically aren't any that are standardized or enforceable.⏎⏎We conducted a policy review across 47 of our affiliated dental clinics in 2023. 68% of clinic administrators said they didn't have sufficient guidance on how AI-assisted diagnostic tools were even handling demographic variables — things like race, primary language, income level. That's not a gap at the margins. That's the majority of our network operating blind on a question that directly affects whether these tools are helping or quietly skewing care recommendations for our patient population. And when I say "our patient population," I mean predominantly Medicaid-enrolled, predominantly low-income, a significant portion of whom are Black Mississippians who already face measurable disparities in diagnosis and treatment without any AI involved.⏎⏎What worries me about the framing of "accelerating adoption" is that it assumes the main problem is slowness. But there's a documented pattern here that HHS should reckon with directly. The pulse oximetry failures in patients with darker skin tones weren't a theoretical risk — they were real patients receiving inaccurate oxygen readings while clinicians trusted the device. The research on this goes back years and the problem persisted because the tool appeared objective. That's the specific danger with AI: when an algorithm produces a recommendation, it doesn't come with a visible margin of error or a notation that it was trained on data that underrepresented your patient population. It just looks like an answer.⏎⏎I'm not saying AI tools have no place in clinical care. Some of our clinic administrators are genuinely interested in what AI might do for scheduling, for early screenings, for documentation burden. But there's a real difference between deploying those tools with meaningful bias auditing requirements in place versus deploying them and hoping someone catches the problems after the fact.⏎⏎The thing that's hard to explain to policymakers who aren't embedded in these settings is how authority works on the ground. When an algorithm flags a patient as lower-risk for a condition, clinicians — especially overworked clinicians in understaffed FQHCs — are going to weight that. Not because they're not good clinicians, but because they're managing 30 patients a day and the tool is supposed to be helping them. If the tool was trained on data that systematically undercoded severity in Black patients (which has been documented in sepsis prediction contexts), then "accelerating adoption" just means spreading that error faster and wider before anyone has the institutional capacity to identify it.⏎⏎What I'd ask HHS to consider is making pre-deployment bias auditing a condition of any federal support or incentive for AI tools used in clinical settings — and specifically requiring that those audits include performance data broken out by race, language, and payer status. Not just once at approval, but on some regular interval. Our patients don't have the luxury of being in the version of the healthcare system where problems get noticed quickly.⏎⏎Also, and I recognize this is a separate issue but it matters: fewer than 20% of our participating facilities reported that patients received any kind of clear explanation of when algorithmic tools were influencing their care. SYNTH-0043♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Thomas Smith♔Salesforce♔♔♔♔♔Salesforce Health & Life Sciences | HHS AI RFI Response | Page 1⏎⏎Thomas Smith, VP Regulatory Affairs⏎Salesforce, Inc.⏎San Francisco, CA / Las Vegas, NV⏎⏎Submitted via regulations.gov⏎⏎My focus in this comment is specifically on the question of how HHS proposes to assess distributional impacts of AI adoption across patient populations — because frankly, what I'm not seeing in the RFI framing is a serious engagement with what happens when the same technology gets deployed very differently depending on where a patient receives care.⏎⏎I want to be direct about where I'm coming from. Salesforce works with health systems across the country, and through our customer relationships we have direct visibility into how AI tools actually get operationalized at the facility level. That visibility has made me considerably more skeptical of the "democratization of AI" framing than I might otherwise be. The phrase implies that broader access equals better outcomes for everyone. I'm not sure that follows, and the data we're seeing from health system customers who are deploying AI imaging and clinical decision support tools suggests the gap between what AI adoption looks like at a well-resourced academic medical center versus a critical access hospital in rural Nevada is substantial enough to warrant real concern.⏎⏎Salesforce Health & Life Sciences | HHS AI RFI Response | Page 2⏎⏎Our concern is specifically about the substitution dynamic. When a large integrated health system in a major metro deploys an AI diagnostic tool, it typically does so alongside existing clinical staff — the AI is genuinely augmenting physician review. When a small or under-resourced facility adopts the same tool, the economic logic often pushes in a different direction. AI becomes a way to operate with fewer clinical staff, not a way to make existing staff more effective. The patients in those settings — who are disproportionately elderly, low-income, have more complex comorbidities, are more likely to be non-English speakers, more likely to have disabilities affecting their ability to self-advocate — those patients end up receiving the most automated care with the least human oversight. I keep coming back to this when I read the RFI's emphasis on "accelerating adoption" as though speed is the primary variable HHS should be optimizing for.⏎⏎What I'm asking HHS to consider, and this relates specifically to the questions around regulatory frameworks for AI governance, is whether any new adoption framework should include mandatory equity impact assessment as a precondition rather than an optional or post-hoc compliance element. Not a checkbox exercise. An actual structured assessment of whether a given deployment is functioning as augmentation or substitution, and for which patient populations, before reimbursement or certification pathways are opened up.⏎⏎Salesforce Health & Life Sciences | HHS AI RFI Response | Page 3⏎⏎I recognize there are arguments on the other side of this. Our industry partners have raised legitimate concerns about compliance cost as a barrier to adoption, and those aren't trivial — in our own customer advisory work we've seen compliance preparation costs for AI deployment running into the range of $80,000+ per facility per payer contract, which does create real access problems for smaller providers. I'm not dismissing that. But the answer to compliance cost burden is to make the required assessments more efficient and standardized, not to waive them for the populations that face the highest operational risk from AI failure.⏎⏎HHS has existing statutory civil rights obligations under Section 1557 and related authorities. Accelerating AI adoption without a framework that operationalizes those obligations in the context of algorithmic decision-making isn't just a policy gap — it creates legal exposure for the Department and for the health systems relying on HHS guidance to structure their own liability frameworks. The liability question is genuinely unsettled right now, particularly for AI outputs that don't fit cleanly into the FDA medical device classification structure, and I've had multiple conversations with health system compliance officers who are making deployment decisions essentially in the dark because there's no authoritative federal guidance on where responsibility sits when an AI-assisted diagnosis contributes to an adverse outcome.⏎⏎I'd also note — and this is maybe a separate issue but I think it's related — that the documentation and audit trail requirements for AI-assisted clinical decisions vary enormously by payer right now, and that inconsistency is itself creating a two-tier problem where larger systems with dedicated regulatory staff can navigate the variation and smaller systems⏎⏎Salesforce Health & Life Sciences | HHS AI RFI Response | Page 4⏎⏎simply can't absorb the administrative load. Standardizing those requirements across payers, ideally through CMS guidance, would do more to actually democratize access to AI-assisted care than almost anything else HHS could do in the near term.⏎⏎tssmith@salesforce.com⏎https://www.salesforce.com/industries/healthcare/[broken]⏎⏎The equity assessment framework we're describing doesn't need to be designed from scratch — there are existing models in the algorithmic accountability literature, and organizations like the Coalition for Health AI published draft guidance in 2023 that could be adapted. What's missing is HHS making clear that these assessments are a condition of participation rather than a best practice.♔♔2026-01-30T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-30T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-24T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-22T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔AI-powered care risks becoming a euphemism for cheaper, thinner, less attentive care that is disproportionately imposed on elderly, poor, disabled, and otherwise vulnerable patients who already face systemic barriers.♔industry♔medium♔hopeful♔Nevada♔VP of regulatory affairs♔56♔A 2024 internal survey of 47 health system customers deploying MediView XR's AI-assisted imaging tools found that 63% cited inconsistent payer documentation requirements as their primary barrier to scaling AI-driven workflows, with compliance preparation costs averaging $84,000 per facility per payer contract. SYNTH-0047♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Margaret Perez♔Association of American Indian Physicians♔♔♔♔♔Association of American Indian Physicians⏎Comments Submitted to HHS Office of the Deputy Secretary⏎RE: Health Sector AI Request for Information — Accelerating AI Adoption in Clinical Care⏎⏎Margaret Perez, Executive Director⏎www.aaip.org | mperez@aaip.org⏎⏎---⏎⏎The framing of this RFI troubles me. "Accelerating adoption" is the premise, and then the questions flow from there. But for the communities we serve — American Indian and Alaska Native patients, many of whom are already navigating Indian Health Service facilities that are underfunded, understaffed, and geographically isolated — the question of how fast to move is not a technocratic one. It is a question about who gets hurt when the speed of deployment outpaces the development of any meaningful accountability structure.⏎⏎I want to focus specifically on one area where I think HHS has both an existing regulatory obligation and a documented failure to act: AI-driven prior authorization and claims denial systems used by insurers, including Medicare Advantage plans that are federal contracts, not private arrangements. This is where I keep coming back when I read through the RFI questions about barriers and opportunities.⏎⏎What we are seeing, and what our member physicians are reporting to us, is not a future risk. It is happening now. AI systems are being used to deny medically necessary care at a scale that no human review team could match, and that is precisely the point — the volume is the product. A system that can issue 50,000 denials in the time it would take a human reviewer to process 200 is not an efficiency tool in any neutral sense. It is a mechanism for making the denial of care the default outcome for people who do not have the time, English fluency, health literacy, or frankly the energy to fight back.⏎⏎Our member physicians who treat Native patients in rural Missouri and across the plains states tell us that appeals processes are written as if the patient has a lawyer on retainer and unlimited working hours. I don't think that's an accident.⏎⏎In October 2023, the Senate Finance Committee released findings showing that certain Medicare Advantage plans were using algorithmic tools to deny post-acute care claims at rates far exceeding what physician reviewers would have approved. One plan's denial rate for skilled nursing facility stays was something like 85 percent higher after the AI tool was implemented than it had been in the prior period. Those are not abstractions. Those are elderly patients, many of them Native elders who have survived generations of inadequate federal health care, being sent home before they are medically ready because an algorithm said so and nobody with a medical license ever looked at the file.⏎⏎HHS has authority here. CMS has existing tools under Medicare Advantage regulations that could require, right now, that any adverse coverage determination generated by or with the assistance of an automated system receive independent physician review before it is final. That is not a new regulatory theory. It is a narrower version of requirements that already exist in some contexts. What is missing is the will to enforce it or extend it.⏎⏎I want to be honest: I do think AI can do good things in clinical care. Diagnostic support tools that help an IHS physician in a clinic with no specialist backup — yes, I see the value. I'm not opposed to the technology categorically. But the RFI treats prior authorization AI and clinical decision support AI as if they belong in the same conversation about "acceleration," and they don't. One helps clinicians make better decisions. The other helps payers make more denials faster. These are not comparable and HHS should not be applying the same framework to both.⏎⏎What I am asking HHS to do in this rulemaking process is to separate those categories explicitly, and to commit that any action taken to accelerate AI adoption in insurance functions — prior auth, claims processing, utilization management — will be conditioned on mandatory human physician review requirements before any denial is finalized. Not after. Before.⏎⏎The appeals data matters here too. We know that the vast majority of patients who receive denials never appeal. For our patient populations the number is almost certainly worse than the national average, because the barriers are higher — language, geography, distrust of institutions, lack of awareness that an appeal is even possible. So when HHS looks at appeal overturn rates to assess whether the system is working, it is only seeing the small fraction of patients who had enough support to navigate the process. The harm to everyone else is invisible in that data, but it is not actually invisible to the physicians who treat these patients.⏎⏎I would also ask HHS to look at the specific question of validation across indigenous populations. AI systems trained predominantly on data from large urban academic medical centers are going to perform differently — and I mean worse — when applied to patient populations with different disease prevalence, different medication histories, different social determinants. The IHS data infrastructure is not well-integrated into the commercial datasets most AI developers are using. That gap has real implications and I don't see it addressed anywhere in this RFI.⏎⏎If HHS wants comments on specific RFI questions: I am primarily addressing the questions related to regulatory actions and reimbursement policy, specifically the questions about what barriers exist and what role HHS should play in governance. The governance question is not really answered by more voluntary frameworks or industry self-attestation. We have had that approach in health IT for a long time and the equity gaps have not closed.⏎⏎I can provide additional documentation from our member survey data if that would be useful to the Department. Our contact information is above though I should note the email listed may have a response delay of several days given our current staffing.♔♔2026-01-16T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-16T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-24T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-22T00:00:00Z♔3♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔AI-driven insurance decisions and prior authorization systems automate the denial of care in ways that are opaque, difficult to appeal, and disproportionately harmful to vulnerable patients.♔advocacy_group♔medium♔concerned♔Missouri♔executive director♔36♔A 2023 survey of 1,200 dental practices in our network found that 47% reported serving patient populations where more than half of individuals identified as belonging to a racial or ethnic minority group, and nearly 60% of those practices expressed uncertainty about whether AI-assisted diagnostic tools had been validated across diverse demographic groups. SYNTH-0048♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Donald Hill♔♔♔♔♔♔My client — I'll call him Marcus, which isn't his real name — spent the better part of 2022 being told his chest pain and fatigue were anxiety. The marketing language always runs ahead of the reality.⏎⏎What I want to respond to specifically is the question in this RFI about barriers to adoption and what HHS can do to speed things up. My honest answer is that I think HHS is asking the wrong question. Or at least asking it too early. They are certified nursing assistants, medical coders, patient navigators, community health workers. These are jobs held disproportionately by women and by people of color. I don't see any mechanism in this RFI that would require health systems to account for what happens to those workers. SYNTH-0057♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Sandra Wright♔National Kidney Foundation♔♔♔♔♔National Kidney Foundation⏎Submitted to HHS Docket re: Health Sector AI RFI - Accelerating AI Adoption in Clinical Care⏎⏎Sandra Wright, Executive Director⏎National Kidney Foundation⏎⏎The question HHS keeps not asking is the one that matters most to me: when an AI-assisted decision harms a patient, who exactly is responsible? I have spent the better part of three months trying to get a straight answer to that question from legal counsel, from vendor representatives, from hospital compliance officers. Nobody will give one. That is not a mystery or an oversight. That is the system working as designed.⏎⏎I want to speak specifically to the reimbursement and deployment acceleration questions in this RFI, because that is where I think the real danger sits. Our patient population is overwhelmingly Medicare and Medicaid-enrolled, many of them managing end-stage renal disease with comorbidities that already make clinical decision-making complicated. When a nephrology practice in, say, Little Rock embeds an AI-assisted care pathway tool into its workflow and that tool misclassifies a patient's GFR trend and delays a referral, the question of who bears liability becomes genuinely unanswerable under current law. The clinician says they followed the tool's recommendation. The hospital says the clinician exercised independent judgment. The vendor contract - and I have read several of these now - contains disclaimers that the output is "informational only" and not a "clinical determination." So we have a patient who was harmed and a circle of parties each pointing at someone else.⏎⏎FDA 510(k) clearance does not fix this. I want to be direct about that because I think the clearance pathway gets cited as though it resolves the accountability question and it simply does not. Clearance tells you that a device is substantially equivalent to a predicate device. It does not tell you whether the tool performs safely on a 68-year-old Black woman with diabetic nephropathy and limited English proficiency in a rural Arkansas clinic. The clinical deployment context is almost never what was tested. And yet once these systems get embedded in EHR workflows and once CMS starts attaching reimbursement incentives to their use, practices will not remove them even when problems surface. The switching costs are too high. The contracts have termination penalties. The staff have reorganized workflows around the tool. I have heard this directly from our affiliated practice networks - not speculation, actual feedback from practice managers who said in so many words that they knew a tool had issues but removal was not realistic.⏎⏎NKF Comments | HHS AI RFI | Page 2⏎⏎That lock-in dynamic is what concerns me most about HHS moving to accelerate adoption right now. The accountability architecture is broken before we have even started scaling. Once reimbursement incentives are attached and workflows are reorganized, the leverage to demand fixes disappears. Vendors know this. A system where nobody is clearly on the hook for clinical harm is not an accident of regulatory gaps that will eventually get tidied up - it is a predictable outcome of an environment where deployment speed is incentivized and liability exposure is contractually minimized.⏎⏎What I am asking HHS to do before issuing any acceleration framework is to answer, in writing, with enforceability: who bears legal responsibility when an AI tool embedded in a clinical workflow contributes to patient harm? Not "the clinician retains responsibility" as a general principle, because that answer has already proven inadequate in contexts where clinicians reasonably relied on cleared, institutionally mandated tools. Something more specific than that.⏎⏎We submitted a formal set of related questions to the Office of the Assistant Secretary for Technology Policy on March 14, 2024 and have not received a substantive response. SYNTH-0060♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔George Garcia♔MedAssets♔♔♔♔♔MedAssets Comments | HHS AI RFI | Docket HHS-2024-AI-001 | Page 1⏎⏎⏎Submitted on behalf of MedAssets, Inc.⏎Submitted by: George Garcia, VP Regulatory Affairs⏎Date: January 2025⏎⏎⏎Regarding the RFI questions on regulatory frameworks and public-private collaboration:⏎⏎I want to focus on something that I don't think is getting enough attention in this conversation, which is the structural risk of what happens after adoption, not during it.⏎⏎Our internal compliance team spent over 340 staff hours in Q3 2024 alone trying to reconcile conflicting payer standards across three major insurers for AI-assisted documentation and triage tools we'd already deployed. That experience is what brings me to this comment. The problem wasn't that we lacked clarity about whether a tool was safe. The problem was that no one could tell us whether it was reimbursable, whether liability attached to the vendor or to us, and whether the billing codes we were using would survive a payer audit. That ambiguity is real and it costs money, but more importantly it costs time that clinicians and compliance staff don't have.⏎⏎What worries me about this RFI is that HHS seems to be approaching AI adoption as primarily an acceleration problem. Get the tools deployed faster, reduce friction, build public-private partnerships. I understand the appeal of that framing. But I keep thinking about what happened with EHR consolidation and I'm not sure we've fully absorbed the lesson there.⏎⏎By 2015, the market had effectively consolidated around a small number of vendors — Epic, Cerner, a few others — and the switching costs had become so large that most health systems were locked in regardless of how those vendors performed or what they charged. The federal incentive structure, Meaningful Use in particular, drove rapid adoption before interoperability standards were mature enough to prevent that lock-in. Now we're looking at AI tools that don't just touch documentation. They touch triage logic, prior authorization workflows, clinical decision support, and increasingly the billing layer itself. If the same pattern plays out here, the exit costs won't be a painful but manageable migration. They'll be functionally impossible to absorb for a mid-sized organization like ours.⏎⏎MedAssets Comments | HHS AI RFI | Docket HHS-2024-AI-001 | Page 2⏎⏎⏎The RFI mentions public-private collaborations and federal data partnerships as mechanisms for accelerating adoption. I'd ask HHS to think carefully about who benefits from those arrangements structurally. Large vendors with existing federal relationships and the staff to participate in extended comment and working group processes will shape those frameworks in ways that smaller competitors and publicly-developed alternatives simply cannot match. That's not speculation. Our own policy working group identified this dynamic across 9 of 11 stakeholder sessions we ran in Q2 2024 — liability uncertainty was the top barrier, but right behind it was concern about being forced into vendor ecosystems that clinical staff hadn't chosen and couldn't easily exit.⏎⏎I'm not opposed to HHS taking action here. The reimbursement ambiguity is genuinely damaging and needs to be resolved. But the sequencing matters a lot. If we get broad adoption incentives before we have functioning interoperability requirements and vendor-neutral reimbursement standards, we're going to be right back in 2015 except the embedded systems will be making clinical recommendations in real time rather than just storing notes.⏎⏎One specific thing I'd ask HHS to consider: before issuing any guidance that ties reimbursement to specific AI capabilities or workflow integrations, require that those capabilities be achievable through open APIs with published technical specifications, not just through a particular vendor's implementation. That one requirement, if enforced, would do more to prevent the lock-in problem than any amount of post-hoc interoperability rulemaking.⏎⏎I recognize that's easier to say than to implement and I don't have a clean answer for how you'd verify compliance without adding a new certification burden on top of everything else our compliance staff is already managing. The 340 hours I mentioned earlier was for one quarter, three payers, and two product categories. We have seven product categories currently in deployment and we're adding more.♔♔2026-02-09T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-09T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-24T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-22T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔AI adoption in healthcare creates vendor dependency, market concentration, and regulatory capture that will be nearly impossible to reverse once embedded.♔industry♔medium♔urgent♔Utah♔VP of regulatory affairs♔47♔A 2024 internal review of our technology vendor partnerships identified that 68% of AI-integrated tools deployed across our clinic network fell into ambiguous classification categories — neither clearly qualifying as medical devices nor fitting neatly into existing reimbursement frameworks. SYNTH-0062♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Susan Robinson♔Leadership Conference on Civil and Human Rights♔♔♔♔♔My work is with families in St. Louis and Kansas City who are already getting worse care than their wealthier, whiter counterparts, and that's before we add algorithms into the mix. That's a device that's been in use for decades. We failed to catch it for decades. Now we're about to deploy diagnostic AI systems trained on that same flawed historical data, at much larger scale and with much less transparency about how the outputs are generated, and we're calling that acceleration.⏎⏎What I hear from clinicians in underserved clinics in Missouri — and I have talked to a lot of them, through our work connecting health advocacy to broader civil rights organizing — is that these lower-cost AI systems often come without any real documentation about what populations the training data came from. The facilities that serve Medicaid patients and uninsured patients are the ones buying the budget tools. The facilities with the resources to vet vendors and demand bias audits are not the ones where our communities are getting care. That's not a hypothetical — that's how these systems are actually deployed.⏎⏎The other piece I keep coming back to is informed consent. If a hospital in Missouri is using an AI prior authorization tool that has a racial disparity baked into its denial rates — and there is documented evidence this is happening in commercial insurance prior auth systems — the patient doesn't know that. SYNTH-0065♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Thomas Hernandez♔National Partnership for Women and Families♔♔♔♔♔The framing of this RFI frustrates me in a way I want to try to articulate clearly, because I think it's doing something that deserves pushback before we even get to the specific questions.⏎⏎HHS is asking how to "accelerate" AI adoption in clinical settings, and embedded in that word is an assumption that speed is the right goal. The question I keep returning to is: accelerating for whose benefit? Because when I look at where AI is actually being deployed in health systems right now, the pattern is pretty consistent. It's being used to increase patient throughput, to flag claims for denial faster, and to monitor how long nurses spend at bedsides. Those are labor productivity and cost-extraction applications. They are not, in any meaningful sense, burnout reduction tools for clinicians. The framing in this RFI that AI adoption is about helping overworked clinicians is, I think, sincerely misleading, and I say that as someone who works in advocacy and spends a lot of time reading the more carefully worded version of these arguments.⏎⏎Our organization works closely with the Psychotherapy Action Network, and data they collected from roughly 1,200 practitioners in 2023 showed that 68% had encountered patients who expressed real concern about how mental health data might be used by automated systems. And what was striking wasn't just the number, it was who was opting out. Patients from lower-income backgrounds and communities of color were substantially more likely to decline digital intake processes when any algorithmic involvement was disclosed to them. That's not a consent form design problem. That's a structural trust problem that you will not fix by updating a notice-and-access requirement.⏎⏎I want to focus on one specific thing in this RFI, which is the question about reimbursement incentives and what role Medicare and Medicaid payment policy should play in accelerating adoption. My concern here is that tying reimbursement to AI use creates an adoption mandate in practice even when it's framed as optional. Hospitals and health systems operating on thin margins will read financial incentives as pressure, and they will respond by deploying whatever tools qualify for those incentives fastest. The vendors who benefit from this are already well-capitalized. The workers who absorb the downside are disproportionately women and workers of color in administrative, intake, and support roles that AI tools specifically target for automation.⏎⏎There's something odd about HHS framing this as a health equity opportunity when the labor market effects of the automation being discussed will land hardest on the same demographic groups the Department says it wants to protect as patients. I'm not sure those two things can be resolved by adding an equity consideration to an otherwise unchanged incentive structure.⏎⏎I'd also flag that the "clinician override" question is more complicated than it looks. The 2024 convening where the Psychotherapy Action Network presented its findings included a pretty frank discussion of how algorithmic recommendations function in practice in under-resourced settings. Clinicians technically retain override authority in most frameworks. But when a system flags a treatment as outside protocol and that flag is tied to billing or quality metrics, the override is nominal. The institutional pressure is real and it runs in one direction.⏎⏎HHS should at minimum require, before any reimbursement incentive attaches to AI tools in clinical settings, independent audits of labor impact disaggregated by race and gender of the workforce affected, and separate audits of patient-facing outcomes disaggregated the same way. Not self-reported vendor data. Independent. And those audits should be public, not submitted to the agency and then summarized in a guidance document two years later.⏎⏎I recognize that's a harder ask than putting together a voluntary framework with an equity checkbox. But the softer version of this has been tried repeatedly and it does not produce accountability, it produces documentation of stated intentions.⏎⏎One more thing I keep thinking about: the RFI mentions reducing administrative burden several times as a rationale. I want to know specifically what that means when a hospital system automates prior authorization review. Because from where I sit, the administrative burden being reduced is a job, and that job is held by a person, and that person is statistically likely to be a woman of color working in a mid-size hospital in a state without strong labor protections. "Burden reduction" is a real thing in the aggregate but it is not evenly distributed and the RFI doesn't grapple with that at all.⏎⏎Thomas Hernandez⏎Research Director, National Partnership for Women and Families⏎Concord, NH♔♔2026-02-10T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-10T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-24T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-22T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔AI in healthcare threatens to be used not to reduce clinician burnout but to justify staffing cuts, intensify surveillance, automate denials, and devalue professional expertise.♔advocacy_group♔medium♔frustrated♔New Hampshire♔research director♔30♔A 2023 survey of 1,200 psychotherapy practitioners affiliated with the Psychotherapy Action Network found that 68% had encountered patients who expressed significant concerns about how their mental health data might be used by automated or AI-assisted systems. SYNTH-0072♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Sarah Jones♔Tufts University School of Medicine♔♔♔♔♔My work over the past several years has focused on how health systems adopt new technologies, and this RFI raises questions that I think are being framed in a way that should worry anyone who has spent time studying that process. The framing assumes that acceleration is the goal, and then asks how to achieve it. That is not a neutral starting position.⏎⏎Let me focus specifically on the question of what evidence standards should accompany accelerated adoption, because I think this is where the sequencing problem is most visible and most consequential.⏎⏎There is a reasonably well-documented gap between how clinical AI systems perform in controlled validation environments and how they perform once deployed at scale across heterogeneous patient populations. My colleagues at the ACR Data Science Institute published survey findings in 2024 showing that among 612 radiology department chairs and research directors, 68% reported that the peer-reviewed evidence base was insufficient to support adoption decisions. Fewer than 12% of validation studies indexed between 2021 and 2023 included prospective, multi-site data. These are not fringe concerns from AI skeptics. These are radiologists who want useful tools and are saying the evidence is not there yet.⏎⏎What concerns me about this RFI is that it seems to treat that evidentiary gap as a problem of access or deployment speed rather than as a signal that we may be moving prematurely. The implicit argument appears to be: if we can get reimbursement structures aligned and regulatory pathways smoothed, adoption will happen faster, and then real-world evidence will accumulate. I understand the logic. But healthcare technology adoption does not work that way in practice. Once a system is reimbursed and embedded in clinical workflows, the practical costs of reversing that decision become enormous. Vendors build around it. Hospitals train staff around it. EHR integrations get written. The question of whether the technology was actually doing what we thought it was doing gets progressively harder to ask, not easier.⏎⏎I am not arguing that AI in clinical care has no promise. Some applications clearly do. But "some applications show promise" and "HHS should act to accelerate adoption broadly" are very different propositions, and I am not sure the second one follows from the first.⏎⏎There is also something uncomfortable about the way this RFI was structured that I want to name directly. The question of whether society wants medicine organized around AI-assisted decision-making at this scale, under these conditions of evidentiary uncertainty, with liability rules still largely unresolved, is a political and moral question. It belongs to democratic deliberation. An RFI that asks how to accelerate something rather than whether to accelerate it is, at least partly, bypassing that question. I recognize HHS has a mandate to think about technology adoption and that this is a legitimate area of agency activity. But there is a difference between asking "what would responsible adoption look like" and asking "how do we move faster," and this document tilts toward the latter in ways that I think deserve pushback.⏎⏎The liability question in particular seems underexamined here. When an AI-assisted diagnostic tool contributes to a missed diagnosis, who is responsible? The vendor? The hospital? The clinician who relied on the recommendation? Until there is some coherent answer to that question, the incentive structure for developers to invest in rigorous post-market surveillance is weak. Accelerating reimbursement pathways before resolving that question doesn't solve the governance problem, it just means more systems get deployed under the same uncertain liability conditions.⏎⏎I would ask HHS to consider whether a more useful framing for the next phase of this work would be to ask what conditions need to be met before acceleration is warranted, rather than assuming acceleration is warranted and working backward to the conditions. That reordering matters. The 2024 NAM working group on AI in health care raised related concerns and I think their interim findings are worth revisiting before this process moves further toward implementation guidance.⏎⏎One practical thing I would suggest: HHS should consider whether any new reimbursement pathways for clinical AI tools should be explicitly time-limited and conditioned on post-market surveillance requirements with teeth, not just reporting requirements that accumulate in a database nobody is resourced to analyze.♔♔2026-01-11T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-11T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-24T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-22T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔Society has the right to decide that some domains should remain stubbornly human, and once AI is embedded in healthcare it becomes practically and politically impossible to remove — making the decision to accelerate now a decision that forecloses future democratic choice.♔academic♔medium♔supportive♔New Mexico♔professor of health policy♔53♔A 2024 ACR Data Science Institute survey of 612 radiology department chairs and research directors found that 68% reported insufficient peer-reviewed evidence on real-world AI performance to support adoption decisions, with reproducibility concerns cited as the leading barrier in 43% of responses. Our analysis of 214 published clinical AI validation studies indexed between 2021 and 2023 further identified that fewer than 12% included prospective, multi-site performance data reflective of diverse patient populations. These findings informed ACR's formal recommendations submitted to the National Academy of Medicine's AI in Health Care working group in early 2024, underscoring the persistent gap between controlled benchmark results and deployable clinical evidence.♔The decision to accelerate AI adoption in healthcare is not merely a technical or administrative question — it is a political and moral question about what kind of medicine society wants, and that question belongs to democratic deliberation, not to an agency acting under industry-aligned pressure to move fast. | Healthcare technology adoption has historically functioned as a one-way ratchet: once embedded in reimbursement structures, clinical workflows, and institutional infrastructure, systems become practically irreversible regardless of their performance — meaning that accelerating now, before governance frameworks exist, forecloses future democratic choice in ways that cannot be undone.♔A health policy researcher argues that HHS is inverting the proper order of technology governance by asking democratic institutions to ratify an acceleration agenda before the oversight mechanisms, liability rules, and evidence standards that would make such acceleration legitimate have been established — and that the irreversibility of healthcare technology adoption makes this sequencing error not merely procedural but catastrophic.♔♔True♔♔702♔-1♔♔0
SYNTH-0073♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Rebecca Ramirez♔Stryker Corporation♔♔♔♔♔Stryker Corporation | HHS Health Sector AI RFI | Docket No. HHS-2024-AI-001 | Page 1⏎⏎⏎My frustration with this RFI is not what people might expect from someone in my position. I have spent the better part of two decades at Stryker navigating FDA clearance pathways, payer coverage determinations, and the patchwork of state-level requirements that govern how our devices and software products reach patients. I am not someone who reflexively argues for slower regulation. What I am is someone who has watched, repeatedly, what happens when deployment outpaces governance — and what concerns me about this particular RFI is that HHS seems to be treating acceleration as the goal rather than as a potential output of getting the foundational architecture right first.⏎⏎The RFI asks HHS to pull regulatory, reimbursement, and research levers simultaneously. That framing assumes those levers can be safely deployed before anyone has resolved the governance questions that would tell you in which direction to pull them. Liability classification for non-device AI is the clearest example I can give. Our own internal analysis found that ambiguity in that space delayed commercial deployment for our non-SaMD AI product line by an average of 8.3 months per product. That is real cost, real delay. But the answer to that problem is not to embed those products in federal reimbursement structures while the liability questions are still unresolved — the answer is to resolve the liability questions. Once a product category is attached to a CMS billing code, the political economy around that code becomes extremely difficult to change. I have seen this with other device categories. The reimbursement structure calcifies, the vendors build business models around it, and course correction becomes operationally and politically very expensive even when the underlying clinical evidence turns out to be weaker than expected.⏎⏎Stryker Corporation | HHS Health Sector AI RFI | Docket No. HHS-2024-AI-001 | Page 2⏎⏎⏎The RFI does not ask directly about liability frameworks, which is itself telling. It asks about reimbursement pathways, regulatory modernization, and R&D investment — all of the deployment-facing questions — without first asking what accountability structure should govern the AI once it is embedded in clinical workflows. That sequencing concern is not abstract. In our 2024 Annual Health AI Policy Forum in September, stakeholders from across the industry — payers, developers, health systems — ranked regulatory harmonization and liability clarification as the top two policy priorities. Not faster reimbursement. Harmonization and clarification. The industry is telling HHS what it actually needs, and this RFI is asking a different question.⏎⏎I want to be careful here because I recognize the other side of this. There are genuinely beneficial AI applications sitting in development queues right now that could improve patient outcomes, and regulatory delay has real costs too. I am not arguing for a moratorium. What I am arguing is that HHS should be honest about the sequencing problem. Establishing unified audit standards, resolving the SaMD/non-SaMD classification boundary for AI, and clarifying malpractice exposure for AI-assisted clinical decisions — those are prerequisites, not parallel workstreams. The HealthScoreAI survey from Q2 2024 found that 54% of AI developers were spending an average of $1.2 million annually on duplicative compliance activities precisely because that unified standard does not exist. Accelerating adoption into that environment does not reduce that burden. It compounds it.⏎⏎Regarding the specific RFI question about reimbursement pathway modernization: CMS should not establish new AI-specific billing codes or coverage categories until the Office of the Inspector General and ONC have jointly published guidance on audit standards for AI-generated clinical recommendations. The sequencing matters. Creating a reimbursement pathway before the audit standard exists means the audit standard, when it eventually arrives, will have to accommodate an already-embedded commercial infrastructure rather than being designed on its own merits. That is exactly the ratchet effect I am describing. Once the billing codes exist and vendors have⏎⏎Stryker Corporation | HHS Health Sector AI RFI | Docket No. HHS-2024-AI-001 | Page 3⏎⏎⏎built revenue projections around them, the political pressure on any subsequent audit or accountability rulemaking becomes enormous. I have watched this dynamic play out in the IVD space and I expect it to be worse with AI because the technology changes faster than any coverage determination process is designed to handle.⏎⏎The question of how much AI should be present in clinical decision-making is not a question HHS can answer unilaterally or should try to answer under acceleration pressure. It involves tradeoffs that patients, clinicians, and the public have not been given a meaningful opportunity to deliberate. I say this as someone who genuinely wants to see well-governed AI reach patients — the absence of that deliberation is a long-term risk to the public legitimacy of health AI that will ultimately affect every company in this space, including ours.⏎⏎Rebecca Ramirez⏎Vice President, Regulatory Affairs⏎Stryker Corporation⏎Kalamazoo, MI 49002⏎⏎Contact for follow-up questions: regulatory.affairs@stryker.com [submission copy — verify direct contact routing before filing]♔♔2026-02-01T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-01T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-24T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-22T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔Society has the right to decide that some domains should remain stubbornly human, and once AI is embedded in healthcare it becomes practically and politically impossible to remove — making the decision to accelerate now a decision that forecloses future democratic choice.♔industry♔high♔frustrated♔Washington♔VP of regulatory affairs♔58♔A cross-industry survey conducted by HealthScoreAI in Q2 2024, spanning 47 health AI developers ranging from early-stage startups to enterprise platform vendors, found that 68% identified inconsistent payer-side evaluation criteria as their primary barrier to scaling reimbursable AI products, while 54% reported spending an average of $1.2 million annually on duplicative compliance activities driven by the absence of a unified federal standard. SYNTH-0074♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Ashley King♔Electronic Frontier Foundation♔♔♔♔♔The thing that keeps coming back to me when I read this RFI is the gap between what HHS is asking about — how to accelerate AI adoption — and what we're actually seeing happen to patients right now. The Department is soliciting ideas about the future while a very concrete, very present harm is already running at scale. AI-driven prior authorization systems are denying medically necessary care every day, and the patients who are being hurt most are exactly the ones this Department has legal obligations to protect.⏎⏎I work in government affairs and I've spent years watching how policy gets made in health care, and what I'm seeing with AI in the insurance context is unlike anything I've encountered before. The speed is the issue. These systems are processing denial decisions faster than any human reviewer could possibly catch up with, and the appeals burden gets pushed onto patients — many of whom are elderly, don't speak English as a first language, are on Medicaid, or are dealing with a serious illness that makes fighting bureaucratic decisions genuinely impossible. We know from reporting on UnitedHealth's claims review tool — which drew significant congressional scrutiny after a ProPublica investigation documented denial rates climbing sharply after the algorithm was deployed — that this isn't hypothetical. These are real people being told no, frequently, by a system that may have been trained on data that doesn't reflect their demographics or their clinical circumstances.⏎⏎There's a related point from within the urologic care space specifically that I want to flag here. Fewer than one in five AI tool vendors in urology practices can actually provide documentation showing their algorithms were validated against racially and socioeconomically diverse patient cohorts. That's a finding from member survey data presented at the AUA 2024 Annual Scientific Meeting. That is a vendor accountability gap that exists right now, before any further acceleration of adoption. And the patients in the practices most likely to be affected — practices where half or more of patients are rural, low-income, or non-English-speaking — are the ones with the least institutional support to push back when something goes wrong.⏎⏎HHS has authority here that it is not currently using. The question this RFI keeps dancing around is how to get AI adopted faster, but the predicate question — whether AI-generated coverage determinations should require meaningful human review before they result in a denial — is something HHS can act on now. Not after more stakeholder engagement. Not pending further research. The legal hooks exist. Choosing not to use them while simultaneously signaling enthusiasm for accelerating AI integration is a policy choice with foreseeable consequences, and I think the Department should be honest with itself about what it's choosing.⏎⏎I want to be careful not to sound like I'm opposed to AI in health care broadly. I'm not. There are genuinely promising applications and I understand why the Department wants to move in this direction. But the framing of this RFI treats adoption speed as the primary variable to optimize, and I don't think that's the right frame when we're talking about populations that have historically been on the losing end of health system decisions.⏎⏎What I'd specifically ask HHS to address: any action plan coming out of this RFI process should include a mandatory human review requirement for AI-generated prior authorization denials, with documented oversight trails — not just a recommendation, an actual requirement with enforcement teeth. And there should be equity auditing standards for any AI tool used in coverage determinations, with public-facing disclosure of validation data. Not a self-attestation checkbox. Actual documentation, reviewable by researchers and patient advocates.⏎⏎The comment period closes and then there will be a response document, and I've seen enough of those to know how these things go. What I hope doesn't happen is that the response focuses on the exciting applications and treats the accountability questions as something to work out later through sub-regulatory guidance that never quite arrives.⏎⏎Ashley King⏎Director of Government Affairs⏎Electronic Frontier Foundation♔♔2026-01-06T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-06T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-24T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-22T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔AI-driven insurance decisions and prior authorization systems automate the denial of care in ways that are opaque, difficult to appeal, and disproportionately harmful to vulnerable patients.♔advocacy_group♔medium♔resigned♔Oklahoma♔director of government affairs♔56♔A 2024 AUA member survey of 1,800 urologic practices found that 38% reported treating patient populations in which at least half of individuals identified as members of historically underserved groups, including rural residents, non-English speakers, and low-income patients reliant on Medicaid. SYNTH-0075♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔James Flores♔Families USA♔♔♔♔♔Families USA Comments on HHS Health Sector AI Request for Information⏎Accelerating AI Adoption in Clinical Care⏎Submitted by James Flores, Executive Director, Families USA⏎⏎Families USA | HHS AI RFI Comments | Page 1⏎⏎The framing of this RFI bothers me more than any specific question in it. "Accelerating adoption" is not a neutral administrative goal. It is a policy choice about sequencing — and HHS is proposing to get the sequencing backwards.⏎⏎Let me explain what I mean by that, because I think it gets obscured by the optimistic language around AI in clinical care.⏎⏎When a technology becomes embedded in clinical workflow — in how diagnoses get documented, how treatment plans get generated, how prior authorizations get processed — it tends to stay embedded. Healthcare IT has a long track record on this. EHR systems sold to hospitals in the 2010s under meaningful use incentives are still running in 2024, often with the same core architecture, because ripping them out is operationally catastrophic. The vendors know this. The institutions know this. The incentive structures calcify around whatever gets deployed first. That is not a conspiracy, it is just how organizations work.⏎⏎So when HHS asks how it can use reimbursement policy, certification requirements, and research funding to speed AI into clinical settings, the honest translation of that question is: how do we use every lever of federal policy to make AI-embedded medicine the default before a governance framework exists to constrain it? Once that default is established, the political economy of reversing it will be formidable. You will have vendors with contracts, institutions with sunk costs, payers with rate structures built around AI-assisted workflows, and clinicians who have partially deskilled in whatever the AI was doing for them. The ratchet clicks. And the people whose health is at stake — patients, most of them with no idea any of this is happening — had no meaningful voice in whether that was the kind of medicine they wanted.⏎⏎Families USA | HHS AI RFI Comments | Page 2⏎⏎I want to address specifically the question the RFI poses about what regulatory or reimbursement actions could support broader AI adoption across care settings. Our answer is that this question cannot be responsibly answered in isolation from equity data that does not yet exist at the necessary scale.⏎⏎We work closely with federally qualified health centers and safety-net providers. What we hear consistently is that AI diagnostic tools are being marketed to these settings without any meaningful evidence that the tools perform equivalently for their patient populations. A 2023 survey involving roughly 1,200 dental practices found that fewer than 12% had any kind of formal informed-consent process specifically addressing AI-assisted decision-making, and practices serving predominantly Medicaid-enrolled or limited-English-proficient patients were more than 40 percentage points less likely to have received vendor-provided equity audits compared to practices in higher-income markets. Dental care is not the whole picture, but it is a concrete window into what "acceleration" looks like on the ground when governance hasn't caught up.⏎⏎The patients in those practices did not consent to being the subject of an unaudited algorithm. They probably don't know they were. That is not a hypothetical concern about future harms, it is a description of something happening right now.⏎⏎Families USA | HHS AI RFI Comments | Page 3⏎⏎I want to push back on something implicit in the RFI's framing that I do not think gets said directly enough in regulatory comment processes. There is a legitimate values question about how much medicine should be automated that has never been put to the public in any meaningful way. The assumption threading through this RFI — and through a lot of the health AI policy conversation generally — is that more AI in clinical care is obviously desirable and the job of regulators is to smooth the path. But that is a contestable premise.⏎⏎A patient sitting with a doctor who is looking at them, thinking about them, making a judgment call that is genuinely theirs — that is not inefficiency to be optimized away. Some people will prefer AI-augmented care. Some people, if actually asked, would prefer medicine that is slower, more human, and more accountable even if it is less scalable. That is a legitimate preference. It is a values choice that belongs to democratic deliberation, not to an administrative acceleration agenda shaped heavily by the vendors and health systems that stand to benefit from deployment.⏎⏎HHS does not have a mandate from the public to decide that AI-saturated medicine is the destination. I would like to see the Department explain where it thinks that mandate comes from before⏎⏎Families USA | HHS AI RFI Comments | Page 4⏎⏎committing reimbursement policy to a trajectory that, practically speaking, will be very hard to walk back.⏎⏎The specific regulatory ask we would make — if HHS proceeds at all — is a mandatory pre-deployment equity audit requirement tied to any reimbursement pathway for AI clinical decision support, with public reporting of audit results disaggregated by race, language, insurance status, and rurality. Not a voluntary framework. A requirement with teeth. And the audit methodology should be set by HHS in consultation with patient advocates and bioethicists, not by the vendors conducting the audits on their own products.⏎⏎We would also ask that HHS commission independent research on patient preferences regarding AI involvement in their care before any reimbursement expansions take effect. The agency may find the results surprising. We have not seen that research done at any scale that would justify the confidence with which "acceleration" is being treated as a shared public goal.⏎⏎Contact for follow-up questions regarding these comments:⏎jflores@familiesusa.org⏎1201 New York Avenue NW, Suite 1100, Washington, DC 20005⏎⏎The specific timeline for any pre-deployment audit requirement is something we would want to discuss further — whether HHS envisions a phased rollout or a hard effective date matters considerably for how safety-net providers would be able to comply, and we have not seen enough detail in the RFI to know how HHS is thinking about♔♔2026-01-28T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-28T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-24T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-22T00:00:00Z♔3♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔Society has the right to decide that some domains should remain stubbornly human, and once AI is embedded in healthcare it becomes practically and politically impossible to remove — making the decision to accelerate now a decision that forecloses future democratic choice.♔advocacy_group♔medium♔angry♔Connecticut♔executive director♔39♔A 2023 survey of 1,200 dental practices affiliated with the Dental Standards Institute found that 38% reported using or piloting at least one AI-assisted diagnostic or treatment-planning tool, yet fewer than 12% had formal informed-consent protocols specifically addressing algorithmic decision-making. SYNTH-0076♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Anthony White♔Society of Hospital Medicine♔♔♔♔♔Society of Hospital Medicine Comments on HHS Health Sector AI Request for Information: Accelerating AI Adoption in Clinical Care⏎⏎Submitted by Anthony White, Director of Government Affairs⏎Society of Hospital Medicine⏎⏎The question HHS is really asking here is how to accelerate adoption, and I understand the impulse. There are genuine clinical applications where AI has shown promise and hospitalists across our membership have seen some of those firsthand. But I keep coming back to a problem that is already documented, already measurable, and will get significantly worse if the Department treats speed as the primary variable.⏎⏎Our member data, along with data from health equity task force work across several of our partner organizations, shows a consistent pattern: diagnostic AI tools that were trained predominantly on commercially insured patient populations produce lower accuracy rates when applied to Medicaid-enrolled and uninsured patients. This is not a theoretical concern. In six pilot practice settings examined as part of a 2024 health equity audit, the accuracy gap was measurable and in some cases clinically meaningful. The tools themselves were not flagged by the clinicians using them because the outputs looked authoritative. That is actually the part that worries me most. A clinician who disagrees with a consultant's recommendation can push back. It is a lot harder to push back on an algorithm, particularly for hospitalists working overnight with high patient volumes and no immediate access to whoever validated the tool in the first place.⏎⏎Society of Hospital Medicine Comments | HHS AI RFI | Page 2⏎⏎I want to address specifically the RFI's questions around regulatory and reimbursement actions HHS could take to accelerate adoption. The Department is asking the right question in the wrong order. Before reimbursement incentives are structured to reward AI adoption, there needs to be a baseline requirement that tools used in clinical settings have undergone some form of independent bias auditing across demographic subgroups, and that the results of that auditing are disclosed to the clinical sites deploying them. Right now there is no such requirement. A vendor can train a sepsis detection algorithm on data from a tertiary academic medical center serving a predominantly commercially insured population, market it to a safety-net hospital in Gary, Indiana, and there is currently nothing requiring them to disclose that the validation cohort looks nothing like the patient population in Gary.⏎⏎If CMS structures reimbursement incentives that reward hospitals for deploying AI tools before those standards exist, the practical effect is that we will be subsidizing the scaling of biased algorithms into exactly the settings where the patient populations have the least margin for diagnostic error and the fewest resources to detect problems after the fact.⏎⏎I recognize HHS cannot solve the entire AI governance problem in one rulemaking cycle. But there are things the Department could do without waiting for legislation. Conditioning any new reimbursement pathways for AI-assisted clinical tools on vendor disclosure of training data demographics and subgroup performance metrics seems like a reasonable starting point. It does not require a new regulatory structure, it just requires that the Department not build financial incentives before the floor is in place.⏎⏎Society of Hospital Medicine Comments | HHS AI RFI | Page 3⏎⏎One other thing I want to flag, and this may be slightly outside the scope of the RFI but I think it is relevant. The clinical workforce is not prepared to interrogate these tools. Our members are hospitalists, they are skilled clinicians, but they are not data scientists and they should not have to be. The question of how to evaluate whether an AI tool is performing appropriately on their patient population is not a question most hospitalists currently have the training or the institutional support to answer. If HHS is going to accelerate adoption, there needs to be some parallel investment in what I would call practical interpretability, meaning giving clinicians accessible information about how a tool was validated and for whom, in a format they can actually use during a consult at 2 a.m. and not a 40-page technical appendix.⏎⏎Our organization would welcome the opportunity to discuss any of this further with the Department. Contact information for our Government Affairs office is below.⏎⏎Anthony White⏎Director of Government Affairs⏎Society of Hospital Medicine⏎awhite@hospitalmedicine.org♔♔2026-02-10T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-10T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-24T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-22T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔AI systems in clinical settings inherit and amplify racial, socioeconomic, and institutional biases from training data, and their apparent authority makes those biases harder to detect and correct.♔advocacy_group♔medium♔resigned♔Indiana♔director of government affairs♔45♔A 2024 ADA member survey of 6,200 practicing dentists found that 38% reported treating patients from populations with limited English proficiency or significant socioeconomic barriers, yet fewer than 12% of those dentists had received any training on how AI-assisted diagnostic tools account for demographic variation in oral health data. SYNTH-0077♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Christopher Young♔♔♔♔♔♔My doctor, Dr. Harlan Briggs, retired in March of 2023 after 31 years of practice in Caruthersville. For a medication she'd been stable on.⏎⏎That's AI in healthcare right now. That's where we actually are.⏎⏎So I guess what I want to say to HHS is -- before you talk about accelerating anything, can you tell me what you're doing to make sure the doctors who are still out there have a reason to stay?
