SYNTH-0078♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Steven Scott♔NVIDIA Corporation♔♔♔♔♔My concern with this RFI is less about whether AI belongs in clinical care — it clearly has a role — and more about whether HHS has thought carefully enough about the gap between how these tools perform in controlled validation studies versus how they actually behave once deployed across the full range of hospital environments, patient populations, and clinical workflows that exist in the real world.⏎⏎I work in compliance at a company that builds and sells AI infrastructure to health systems. I have a direct financial interest in seeing AI adoption accelerate. I want to be transparent about that. But that's also why I think the governance question matters more than some of the commentary in this space acknowledges. When AI tools fail visibly and publicly in clinical settings, the reputational and liability consequences land on the companies that built and deployed them, not on the agencies that encouraged rapid adoption. So I don't think the industry's interest and the patient safety interest are as opposed as they sometimes get framed.⏎⏎The specific issue I keep coming back to is what gets called "automation bias" in the clinical AI literature. There's a reasonably well-documented body of research showing that when clinicians are given AI-generated recommendations, their independent judgment tends to track those recommendations even when the AI is wrong — sometimes especially when the AI is wrong, because the failure modes of these systems often occur in exactly the edge cases where a fatigued or cognitively overloaded clinician is most likely to defer. The apparent cognitive burden reduction that makes these tools attractive can produce worse net outcomes if the underlying model performance degrades outside its training distribution and nobody is monitoring for that in real time.⏎⏎NVIDIA Corporation Comments | HHS AI RFI | Page 2⏎⏎This isn't a theoretical concern. The sepsis prediction algorithm deployed at University of Michigan Health was the subject of a fairly detailed independent audit published in 2023 in JAMA Internal Medicine that found substantial performance degradation in subgroups that were underrepresented in the training data. That's one well-documented case. The concern I have is that we don't have a systematic mechanism for surfacing these cases before they become patient safety events or litigation triggers.⏎⏎What I'd like to see HHS address specifically — and I recognize this is only one of the questions the RFI is asking — is what post-market surveillance requirements should apply to AI tools that don't meet the FDA's definition of a software as a medical device but are still being used to support clinical decisions. This is the liability ambiguity problem that I hear about constantly from legal and compliance teams across the industry. Right now there's no clear answer. A tool that summarizes a patient chart and flags potential drug interactions might be classified as a clinical decision support tool exempt from FDA oversight, but it's functionally doing something that, if wrong, could cause direct patient harm. The regulatory category and the actual risk profile are not well-aligned.⏎⏎I'm not arguing against HHS's goal here. I think there are real opportunities. But accelerating adoption without closing this surveillance gap first is going to produce the same pattern we saw with EHR rollout — rapid deployment, followed by years of documented unintended consequences, followed by expensive retrospective fixes. The certification and interoperability problems from that era are still not fully resolved and we're already talking about layering AI on top of that infrastructure.⏎⏎Regarding the question about reimbursement pathways specifically: I think payer coverage policy is actually where a lot of the practical deployment decisions get made, and the inconsistency across payers right now is creating a situation where health systems are making AI investment decisions based on anticipated coverage that may or may not materialize. I've seen product deployment timelines slip by six to nine months because a health system was waiting on a coverage determination from a major commercial payer that never came through in the expected form. That's a real cost, and I understand why HHS wants to address it. But I'd want any streamlined reimbursement pathway to include explicit conditions around ongoing performance reporting, not just pre-deployment validation. The problem isn't getting these tools approved. It's knowing whether they're still working the way they were validated to work eighteen months after go-live in a new patient population.⏎⏎One other thing that I don't think gets enough attention: the vendors doing the post-market monitoring need clear guidance on what data they're allowed to use for that monitoring under HIPAA. Right now there's genuine legal uncertainty about whether a vendor can access de-identified encounter data from a deployed model to evaluate drift, even with a BAA in place, and different covered entities have very different interpretations of what's permissible. That's a practical barrier that a clarifying guidance document from OCR could probably address without⏎⏎NVIDIA Corporation Comments | HHS AI RFI | Page 3⏎⏎requiring any rulemaking, and it would meaningfully change what's operationally feasible for ongoing surveillance.⏎⏎Contact: sscott@nvidia.com⏎Submitted on behalf of: NVIDIA Corporation, Compliance Division♔♔2026-02-16T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-16T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-24T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-22T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔AI tools that appear impressive in demonstrations are often brittle in real clinical environments, adding hidden risk while creating false reassurance about safety and effectiveness.♔industry♔high♔hopeful♔Florida♔compliance officer♔46♔A 2024 internal policy analysis conducted across Writerslogic's network of 47 health technology partners found that 68% identified liability ambiguity for non-medical device AI tools as their primary compliance concern, with 54% reporting that inconsistent payer standards had directly delayed product deployment timelines by an average of 8.3 months. SYNTH-0079♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Linda Scott♔University of Alabama at Birmingham School of Medicine♔♔♔♔♔My institution's IRB approved a study last year examining AI-assisted polyp detection errors at three academic endoscopy centers, and what I keep coming back to is not the error rates themselves but what happened when we tried to figure out who was responsible for a specific category of missed lesions that the AI tool had flagged as low-risk. The vendor's contract language — and I have read it, or tried to — essentially said that outputs are "decision support" and that clinical judgment supersedes the tool's recommendation, which means the moment a physician uses the output, the physician owns the outcome. The hospital's credentialing committee took the position that the tool was FDA-cleared and therefore presumptively safe. The gastroenterologist who had used the tool said, reasonably, that he had no way to independently audit its performance in our patient population because the vendor does not provide site-specific accuracy data. So: vendor blames clinician, clinician blames vendor, hospital says FDA cleared it. Nobody is wrong, technically, and nobody is accountable either.⏎⏎That is what this RFI is being submitted into. HHS is asking how to accelerate adoption and the fundamental question of who is legally responsible for AI-caused patient harm has no coherent answer right now. I am not sure HHS fully reckons with that in the framing of this document.⏎⏎The FDA 510(k) clearance process does not evaluate whether a tool performs safely in the deployment context where it actually gets used. A colonoscopy AI trained predominantly on high-volume academic center endoscopy footage from Japan may carry a clearance that says nothing meaningful about its accuracy in a community practice in rural Alabama serving a predominantly Black patient population with different adenoma prevalence rates and different bowel prep quality patterns. I know this because I study this. And yet once a tool has 510(k) clearance, institutions treat that as a green light, liability is diffuse, and the performance validation data that would tell you whether the tool is actually working in your patient population is proprietary and not disclosed.⏎⏎I want to respond specifically to the question in this RFI about what regulatory or reimbursement actions HHS should take to support responsible AI adoption. My answer is that HHS should not accelerate reimbursement incentives for AI-assisted clinical tools until there is a functioning liability framework that tells patients, clinicians, and institutions who is legally responsible when the tool contributes to harm. Right now CMS reimbursement signals create adoption pressure without accountability structure. That combination is genuinely dangerous, not in a rhetorical sense but in the sense that patients will be harmed and will have no identifiable defendant with deep enough pockets to make them whole.⏎⏎And the irreversibility problem is something I don't see discussed enough. Once AI is embedded in a billing workflow or a standard-of-care protocol, removing it is enormously costly even when you have evidence it is underperforming. Institutions will tolerate known performance gaps rather than absorb removal costs. I have seen this with EHR clinical decision support tools that everyone knows are poorly calibrated but nobody turns off because of workflow dependencies. AI diagnostic tools will be the same, probably worse because the vendor relationships are more complex.⏎⏎I don't have a clean policy recommendation that solves all of this. I do think HHS should be requiring, as a condition of any reimbursement recognition, that vendors provide site-level performance data to deploying institutions in a standardized format, and that contracts between vendors and health systems contain explicit liability allocation language that is publicly disclosed in aggregate. Whether that is achievable through existing HHS authority or requires legislative action I genuinely don't know.⏎⏎What I do know is that asking how to make adoption faster before asking who pays when it goes wrong is the wrong sequencing, and I'm not sure the urgency in this RFI's framing is warranted given where the accountability infrastructure actually stands as of early 2025.♔♔2026-02-21T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-21T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-24T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-22T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔Embedding AI in healthcare diffuses responsibility so thoroughly that when errors occur — and they will — no single actor can be held accountable, creating a system where patients are harmed with no meaningful recourse.♔academic♔medium♔angry♔Arizona♔associate professor♔59♔A 2024 survey of ACG member gastroenterologists across 47 academic medical centers found that 68% reported encountering at least one AI-assisted diagnostic or procedural tool in clinical practice within the prior 12 months, yet fewer than 30% indicated they had access to institution-level performance validation data for those tools. SYNTH-0081♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Barbara Nelson♔Mental Health America♔♔♔♔♔My concern with this RFI is not really about whether AI can read a scan or flag a drug interaction. It probably can, sometimes better than an exhausted resident at 2am. Many of them have serious trauma histories. A lot of them have been failed by systems before — involuntary commitments, medication errors, providers who didn't listen. When someone finally gets to a point where they're willing to sit in a chair across from a clinician and say "I'm not okay," something genuinely delicate is happening. I am not being sentimental when I say that. The therapeutic relationship in mental health care is itself part of the treatment. There is peer-reviewed evidence on this going back decades.⏎⏎So when I read language in this RFI about "accelerating adoption" and "reducing barriers," my first question is: barriers for whom? Because from where I sit, the barrier most often described by the people we serve is that they can't get a real human being to take time with them. That's the gap. Not computational speed.⏎⏎I keep thinking about a conversation I had at our statewide provider convening in Columbia last October. A community mental health center director told me her staff had started using an AI documentation tool to reduce note-writing time, which sounds reasonable. But she noticed her clinicians were spending the time they saved staring at screens to review the AI output rather than staying present with patients. The tool was technically reducing documentation burden. The patients noticed something was different. Some of them asked about it directly.⏎⏎That's not an implementation problem you fix with better training protocols. It reflects something about what happens when you insert a recommendation layer between a clinician's attention and a patient's experience.⏎⏎I want to be honest that I don't think all AI in clinical care is harmful. I'm not arguing that. A tool that helps a rural primary care doc in Orangeburg County identify a medication interaction they might have missed — that's genuinely good. But this RFI is asking about acceleration and adoption at scale, and I think HHS needs to be more careful about the specific contexts where AI decision support risks replacing the kind of attentive, responsive human judgment that isn't just nice to have but is clinically functional.⏎⏎The RFI asks about what evidence gaps exist for AI in clinical care. My answer to that is straightforward: we have almost no rigorous evidence on what AI-mediated care does to therapeutic alliance, patient disclosure, treatment adherence among people with histories of institutional distrust, or outcomes in behavioral health settings generally. The literature on AI in clinical settings skews heavily toward imaging, pathology, and other areas where the "patient relationship" variable is easier to bracket out. Mental health is not like that. Psychiatry is not like that. And yet there's nothing in this RFI that suggests those differences are being treated as meaningful design constraints.⏎⏎I would ask HHS to at minimum require that any AI tool seeking expedited reimbursement pathways or certification under this effort demonstrate that its intended use population was meaningfully represented in the evaluation process — and that "performance" metrics include patient-reported experience measures, not just clinical accuracy statistics. The field doesn't have consensus on how to measure that yet, which is itself something the Department could invest in before pushing adoption.⏎⏎There's also the question of informed consent, which I don't think this RFI engages with seriously enough. South Carolina has no state-level requirement that patients be told when an AI tool is influencing their clinical encounter. I've been trying to track whether any other states do and the picture is genuinely murky right now as of early 2024.♔♔2026-01-29T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-29T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-24T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-22T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔Medicine is fundamentally a human relationship built on judgment, context, and trust — not an information-processing problem that AI can solve.♔advocacy_group♔medium♔hopeful♔South Carolina♔policy director♔56♔A 2023 AUA member survey of 2,100 urological practices found that 38% reported uncertainty about how AI-assisted diagnostic tools were trained, and fewer than one in five said their institutions had formal protocols for disclosing algorithmic decision-support use to patients. SYNTH-0082♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Barbara Davis♔♔♔♔♔♔Two winters ago I had a mammogram that came back with what the radiologist described as "an ambiguous cluster of calcifications" in my left breast. SYNTH-0084♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Melissa Clark♔University of New Mexico School of Medicine♔♔♔♔♔My background is in biostatistics, and I've spent the better part of twenty years looking at how performance claims get made for diagnostic and clinical tools — how they're constructed, what datasets they come from, and how often those numbers hold up when the tool moves from the lab that produced it into a different patient population. So when I read this RFI, my first reaction was not enthusiasm. It was a kind of tired recognition.⏎⏎The RFI asks, essentially, what HHS can do across regulatory, reimbursement, and research channels to accelerate AI adoption in clinical care. I want to focus on that word "accelerate" and on what it actually implies when you're using all three levers at once.⏎⏎Here is the thing that doesn't get said enough in these proceedings: embedding a technology in reimbursement structures is not a neutral act that can be undone if the evidence later looks worse than expected. Once AI-assisted diagnostics or AI-supported clinical decision tools are built into what Medicare pays for, and once health systems have restructured their workflows around those tools, and once vendors have locked institutions into multi-year contracts, the political and institutional cost of reversal becomes very large. Larger, almost certainly, than the cost of tolerating whatever harms the tools are producing. I've watched this logic operate in other domains and I think it's underappreciated here. The decision to accelerate adoption now is not separable from the decision to make future course-correction extremely difficult.⏎⏎The evidentiary situation is not reassuring. Data presented at CAP's 2024 Annual Meeting found that fewer than 22% of AI tools reviewed through CAP's Laboratory Improvement Programs had undergone prospective, multi-site validation before clinical implementation. The majority were validated on single-institution retrospective datasets. As a biostatistician I can tell you that this is not a minor methodological quibble — performance degrades substantially when you move from homogeneous training populations to the kind of real-world variation you see across laboratory environments, patient demographics, and imaging equipment. The 22% figure represents tools already in use. HHS is being asked to accelerate the adoption of the other 78%.⏎⏎I want to raise something that I think is actually the deeper concern, even though it's harder to fit into a standard regulatory comment format. There is a prior question before the question of evidence standards, which is: who decided that medicine should be optimized for AI adoption speed in the first place? I don't mean that rhetorically. I mean it as a genuine governance question. A society is entitled to decide, through something resembling actual deliberation, that clinical medicine should be slower, more accountable, and more dependent on human judgment, even if that choice has efficiency costs. The implicit assumption running through this RFI is that speed of adoption is a good we should be removing barriers to. But that assumption hasn't been democratically vetted. It's been made by a combination of industry interest, research enthusiasm, and agency preference.⏎⏎The RFI mentions "governance frameworks" as something HHS will work to develop alongside accelerated adoption. That ordering bothers me considerably. Governance frameworks, liability rules, and audit requirements are not the kind of thing you build on the fly while the technology is already embedded in clinical workflows. They need to precede broad deployment, not trail it.⏎⏎I recognize that some AI applications in clinical settings have genuinely promising evidence behind them and that not every tool in every context deserves the same level of skepticism. I'm not arguing for prohibition. What I'm arguing is that HHS should be honest about what "acceleration" forecloses, and that using reimbursement, regulatory, and research levers simultaneously to drive adoption before governance exists is a choice that deserves far more explicit public justification than this RFI provides.⏎⏎I'd specifically ask HHS to address, in its response to comments, what the reversal mechanism looks like. If tools that are reimbursed under Medicare are later found through post-deployment surveillance to have differential error rates across racial or socioeconomic subgroups — which is not a hypothetical, it is a documented pattern in the AI literature — what is the actual procedural pathway for removing or conditioning that reimbursement? How long does that process take? What evidence threshold triggers it? I have not seen these questions answered anywhere in the RFI framing, and they seem to me to be more important than most of what the RFI does ask.♔♔2026-02-02T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-02T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-24T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-22T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔Society has the right to decide that some domains should remain stubbornly human, and once AI is embedded in healthcare it becomes practically and politically impossible to remove — making the decision to accelerate now a decision that forecloses future democratic choice.♔academic♔medium♔frustrated♔Wisconsin♔biostatistician♔55♔A 2024 CAP member survey of 1,200 pathology practices found that 68% reported encountering AI-assisted diagnostic tools with performance claims derived exclusively from retrospective, single-institution datasets—raising reproducibility concerns when deployed across diverse laboratory environments. SYNTH-0085♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Michelle Perez♔American Academy of Orthopaedic Surgeons♔♔♔♔♔On behalf of the American Academy of Orthopaedic Surgeons, I want to focus specifically on the question of what safeguards HHS intends to require before AI systems are used in coverage determination and prior authorization functions, because that is where we are seeing the most direct and immediate harm to patients right now.⏎⏎AAOS Comments | HHS AI RFI | Health Sector Accelerating Adoption | Page 1⏎⏎The framing of this RFI is largely about acceleration. How do we move faster, adopt more broadly, reduce friction. And there are certainly applications of AI in clinical settings where moving carefully but with some urgency makes sense. We are not opposed to AI tools in healthcare. But the question of prior authorization is different in kind from, say, an imaging analysis tool that a physician reviews before acting on. AI-driven prior auth denials are already functionally final for a large share of patients. The appeals process exists on paper. In practice, for an elderly Medicare patient in rural Arkansas, or a non-English-speaking patient navigating a commercial plan's appeals portal, that process is not meaningfully accessible.⏎⏎We have been tracking insurer use of automated review systems for orthopedic procedures specifically. In 2023, a report from the House Energy and Commerce Committee examined how one major insurer used an AI system to deny post-acute care claims with an internal override rate of less than 0.2% — meaning a human reviewer almost never second-guessed the algorithm. That is not human oversight in any real sense. That is a rubber stamp on an automated decision.⏎⏎AAOS Comments | HHS AI RFI | Health Sector Accelerating Adoption | Page 2⏎⏎What we are asking HHS to do is straightforward: use the regulatory authority you already have to require that any AI-generated denial of a prior authorization request receive a substantive clinical review by a licensed physician before it is issued. Not a checkbox. Not a sign-off after thirty seconds. A real review with documented clinical rationale. This is not a novel idea — it is what the rules have always said should happen, and what AI systems are now making it easy for insurers to circumvent at scale.⏎⏎There is also a transparency problem that the RFI does not fully grapple with. Patients generally have no idea that an algorithm generated the denial they received. They think a doctor looked at their case. HHS should require that any coverage determination substantially influenced by an automated system disclose that fact to the patient in plain language, in their preferred language, and at the time the determination is communicated.⏎⏎The populations most affected by unchecked AI denials — low-income Medicare and Medicaid beneficiaries, people with disabilities, older adults with complex orthopedic needs — are precisely the populations HHS has statutory obligations to protect under civil rights and non-discrimination provisions. Choosing to accelerate AI adoption in insurance functions while leaving those obligations unaddressed is a policy choice with predictable consequences. We have seen what happens when insurers are given efficient new tools to deny care without commensurate accountability structures.⏎⏎AAOS Comments | HHS AI RFI | Health Sector Accelerating Adoption | Page 3⏎⏎I would also note that our member surgeons are spending an enormous amount of time on the back end of these denials — filing peer-to-peer reviews, submitting additional documentation, sometimes multiple rounds. That administrative burden falls on practices too, not just patients, and it disproportionately burdens smaller practices that do not have the staff infrastructure to sustain it. A solo or two-physician orthopedic practice in a rural community does not have a prior auth coordinator on staff the way a large hospital system does.⏎⏎We are happy to provide additional documentation from our membership on the specific procedural categories where AI-driven denials are most concentrated. Knee and hip arthroplasty, spinal decompression, and rotator cuff repair appear consistently in what our members report, though we do not yet have a systematic study we can point to, which is itself a problem — the data on AI denial rates by procedure is largely held by the insurers.⏎⏎Contact: policy@aaos.org⏎⏎The absence of publicly accessible data on how these systems are performing is part of what makes this RFI feel premature in certain respects. HHS is asking how to accelerate adoption before there is even a clear picture of the harm the current wave of adoption has already caused.♔♔2026-02-18T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-18T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-24T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-22T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔AI-driven insurance decisions and prior authorization systems automate the denial of care in ways that are opaque, difficult to appeal, and disproportionately harmful to vulnerable patients.♔advocacy_group♔medium♔concerned♔Arkansas♔campaign director♔44♔A 2024 member survey conducted across 47 community mental health centers serving predominantly low-income and minority populations found that 68% reported insufficient internal capacity to audit or evaluate AI-driven clinical tools for potential bias before deployment. SYNTH-0086♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Sharon Rivera♔United Cerebral Palsy♔♔♔♔♔My work is with families affected by cerebral palsy and other disabilities across New Hampshire, and I have sat in enough care settings over the past decade to know exactly what "accelerating adoption" looks like on the ground for the people I work with. It doesn't look like innovation. It looks like one more system making decisions about people who have very little power to question those decisions.⏎⏎The framing in this RFI — that AI will democratize access to high-quality care — I want to be honest, that framing worries me more than almost anything else in this document. Because I have seen what "democratized" technology actually delivers in practice for low-income and disabled patients in rural New Hampshire. It delivers automation in place of attention. The Concord-area hospital system that serves many of our families has been rolling out algorithmic risk-scoring tools in its outpatient rehabilitation program, and what I keep hearing from families is not that they feel better served. They feel like no one is actually looking at their kid anymore.⏎⏎HHS is asking specifically about what actions it can take to accelerate adoption, and my answer is: that is the wrong question right now, or at least not the first question. The first question is what guardrails need to be in place before you accelerate anything. Our network conducted a survey in 2023 and 68% of affiliated clinicians and patient advocates reported at least one case where an algorithmic screening or risk-scoring tool produced an outcome that put a low-income or racially marginalized patient at a disadvantage. 47 separate documented incidents involving inadequate informed consent when AI tools were introduced in mental health settings. These aren't hypothetical future harms. This is already happening.⏎⏎What I want HHS to specifically address — and I don't see it in the questions as written — is the two-tier problem. Well-resourced patients, patients with private insurance and educated advocates and access to specialists, they are going to get AI as a supplement to human care. A tool that helps their doctor catch something sooner, flag a medication interaction, whatever. That's genuinely useful. But for the patients I work with? The elderly woman on Medicaid in Colebrook who sees a nurse practitioner twice a year? AI is not going to supplement her care. It is going to replace what little care she currently gets. That's not a prediction. That's just the economic logic of how underfunded systems adopt new tools — they adopt them to cut costs, not to add services.⏎⏎HHS has civil rights obligations and equity mandates. I understand that. But those mandates are essentially meaningless if the agency accelerates AI adoption in clinical settings without first requiring something like an equity impact assessment — a real one, not a checkbox — for any AI tool being introduced in settings that predominantly serve Medicaid patients, uninsured patients, patients with disabilities, or patients with limited English. The training datasets underlying a lot of the behavioral health algorithms in wide use right now significantly underrepresent non-English speakers and uninsured populations. Our 2024 Health Equity in Mental Health report documents this pattern in detail and I would encourage the agency to review it.⏎⏎I also want to say something about informed consent because I think it gets treated as a paperwork problem when it is actually a rights problem. When a family I work with sits down with a clinician and that clinician is using an AI diagnostic assist tool, does that family know? Do they have any meaningful ability to ask questions about it, decline it, understand how it works? In the 47 incidents our policy review committee documented, the answer was consistently no. The tool was just there, embedded in the workflow, invisible to the patient.⏎⏎I don't know exactly what the regulatory mechanism should be for fixing that. That's not my expertise. But I do know that if HHS publishes a final rule or action plan coming out of this RFI that doesn't include a concrete requirement around disclosure and consent in AI-assisted clinical encounters, then the agency will have made a choice. A deliberate choice that some patients' right to know what is being used on them is less important than the speed of adoption.⏎⏎One more thing and then I'll stop. Our families often can't afford to get it wrong. A delayed or incorrect diagnosis for a child with cerebral palsy has consequences that compound over years of development. The margin for error is not the same across all patient populations and the people designing these systems, and the people at HHS reviewing this RFI, should be spending more time thinking about what happens at the bottom of the distribution, not the average case.⏎⏎I'm happy to provide additional documentation from our network's survey and incident reports if that would be useful to the review process. SYNTH-0090♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Kenneth Lewis♔University of South Florida Morsani College of Medicine♔♔♔♔♔My work is in biostatistics, and I spend a lot of time reading AI validation studies across clinical domains. What I'm seeing in the literature right now genuinely worries me, and I think HHS needs to hear this before moving forward on accelerating adoption.⏎⏎The reproducibility problem is not abstract. A 2024 survey across ACG member institutions found that 58% of gastroenterology researchers had encountered AI performance claims in clinical literature they couldn't reproduce under real-world practice conditions. The two most commonly cited reasons were inconsistent validation datasets and inadequate reporting of confidence intervals. I work in biostatistics. Those are not minor methodological quibbles. Those are the kinds of failures that mean a tool performs well in a paper and then does something different when a clinician in Billings, Montana is actually using it on a patient.⏎⏎And the subgroup problem is worse. 34% of those same researchers noted that published AI studies in GI endoscopy lacked sufficient sample sizes to support subgroup-level generalizability claims. Which brings me to what I actually want HHS to think hard about, because I don't think the acceleration framing fully grapples with this: when a model is trained on data that already reflects decades of unequal care, and then deployed at scale before anyone has done rigorous bias auditing, you're not just making an error. You're institutionalizing it. The apparent authority of an algorithmic output makes clinicians less likely to question it, not more. There's evidence on this from human factors research and from implementation studies. The bias doesn't disappear into the math, it just becomes harder to see and harder to challenge.⏎⏎I keep coming back to a specific example that stuck with me from a 2023 NEJM paper on a sepsis prediction algorithm deployed across a large health system. The model's performance degraded significantly in Black patients relative to white patients, and the degradation wasn't caught for months after deployment because the post-market surveillance was essentially voluntary and inconsistently applied. That is not a hypothetical failure mode. That is a documented, real harm that happened under conditions very similar to what HHS is now proposing to accelerate.⏎⏎I'm not opposed to AI in clinical care. I want to be clear about that because I think people dismiss this kind of concern as technophobia. The question is whether we're building the evidence infrastructure to catch failures before they scale. Right now we're not. The FDA's current framework for AI/ML-based SaMD doesn't require prospective bias auditing stratified by race, insurance status, or geographic region as a condition of clearance. HHS could change that, or at minimum condition any federal investment or reimbursement incentives on meeting those standards.⏎⏎What I'd specifically ask HHS to consider is making bias auditing requirements, including mandatory stratified performance reporting across demographic subgroups, a precondition for any reimbursement pathway or federal research partnership for clinical AI tools. Not a recommendation. A requirement with teeth. The reporting should include confidence intervals, not just point estimates, and should be updated on a defined schedule after deployment, not just at the time of initial validation.⏎⏎I recognize there are implementation costs here and that smaller health systems may struggle with the infrastructure needed for ongoing post-market surveillance. That's a real tension and I don't want to pretend it isn't. But the alternative, which is moving fast and hoping someone catches the bias problems later, has a track record at this point and it's not good.⏎⏎One more thing I'd flag: the confidence interval issue I mentioned earlier is something HHS could address through research funding priorities. A lot of the AI validation studies getting published right now are underpowered for the claims they're making. If HHS is going to invest in AI research infrastructure, requiring minimum sample size thresholds and mandatory reporting of uncertainty bounds in federally funded AI studies would at least raise the floor on the evidence base. SYNTH-0091♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Donna Mitchell♔National Disability Rights Network♔♔♔♔♔The framing of this RFI concerns me more than any single question it asks. HHS is soliciting input on how to accelerate AI adoption in clinical care, but the question of whether to accelerate — and for whom, and under what preconditions — is being treated as already settled. It isn't.⏎⏎The National Disability Rights Network works directly with safety-net providers and the patients who depend on them, and what we see on the ground does not support an acceleration-first posture. I want to address specifically the RFI's questions around implementation barriers and equity considerations, because I think the agency is looking at those two things separately when they are actually the same problem.⏎⏎Our policy review work, including analysis of incident reports submitted to practice management helplines between 2022 and 2024, consistently shows that AI-related billing and diagnostic discrepancies concentrate in safety-net settings. Not randomly. Not uniformly. In clinics serving Medicaid-enrolled patients, patients with limited English proficiency, patients with cognitive or communication-related disabilities. The patients who are already least positioned to notice when something has gone wrong with a recommendation, least able to push back, least likely to have a care team with enough bandwidth to catch an algorithmic error before it affects their treatment.⏎⏎This isn't an abstract concern about bias in training data, though that matters too. It's about what happens when an under-resourced rural clinic in, say, southwestern Colorado adopts an AI diagnostic tool primarily because they can't hire enough staff. The AI isn't supplementing clinical judgment there. It is substituting for it. And if the tool produces a wrong output for a 71-year-old Medicaid patient whose first language is Spanish and who has a physical disability affecting how she presents symptoms, there is no redundant layer of human review catching that error. The efficiency gain that makes the tool attractive to the clinic is exactly the thing that removes the safety net for the patient.⏎⏎HHS has civil rights obligations under Section 504 and Section 1557 that don't disappear because a new technology is involved. I'm genuinely uncertain whether the Department has done a serious, documented equity impact assessment of what accelerated AI deployment means for these populations before issuing this RFI. If it has, that analysis should be made available for public comment. If it hasn't, then what we are really being asked to comment on is a decision that has already been made without the foundational work that HHS's own equity commitments require.⏎⏎I also want to flag something that I think gets lost in these policy discussions. A 2024 survey of dental practitioners found that 31% had received no formal guidance on disclosure obligations when AI systems inform clinical recommendations. Thirty-one percent. And that's among practitioners who are engaged enough to respond to a survey. The informed consent frameworks that currently exist were not designed with algorithmic decision-making in mind, and patients with cognitive disabilities or limited English proficiency are already among the populations for whom standard consent processes are least adequate. Accelerating deployment before that gap is addressed isn't neutral. It's a choice about who bears the risk.⏎⏎I recognize there are real benefits to AI in clinical care and I don't think NDRN's position is that development should stop. But the RFI question about removing implementation barriers needs to be understood in context. Some of what reads as a "barrier" is actually a protection. The friction involved in documenting how an AI tool performs across demographic subgroups, or in training staff on disclosure obligations, or in conducting pre-deployment equity review — that friction exists for reasons. Before HHS moves to reduce it, I would want to know specifically which barriers are being targeted, whether any of them currently function as protections for the populations I've described, and what replaces them if they are streamlined away.⏎⏎We are happy to provide more detailed input on specific questions and can connect HHS staff with member organizations serving the populations most affected. SYNTH-0093♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Donna White♔♔♔♔♔♔Thirty-eight years of nursing and I've watched health IT go through this same cycle more times than I can count. Someone shows up with a new system that's going to fix everything, everyone gets excited, it gets rolled out before anyone really understands how it performs on actual patients, and then the quiet near-misses start accumulating until something bad enough happens that people pay attention.⏎⏎What I keep coming back to is Ruthie. She's a patient of mine, 79 years old, Nez Perce, I've been seeing her at our clinic in Lewiston for six years now. In March of 2024 she was nearly started on a cardiac medication that the algorithm had flagged as appropriate. The dosing recommendation looked right on the screen. It wasn't right for her. Her daughter was there and she's sharp, she asked questions, and we caught it. But the reason we caught it is not because the system worked. It's because a family member happened to be present and happened to know enough to push back. That's not a safety net. That's luck.⏎⏎What I want HHS to understand is that the gap between how these tools perform in controlled testing and how they perform in a clinic like ours is not a small technical detail to be worked out later. It is the whole problem. Ruthie's situation — her age, her background, the particular way her health history is documented across different systems, the medications she'd been on for years — none of that fit neatly into whatever population the dosing algorithm was built around. The tool didn't know what it didn't know. And the screen didn't say "this recommendation is based on data that may not reflect patients like yours." It just gave a number.⏎⏎The other thing I want to say, and I'm not sure I've seen this addressed directly anywhere in what I've read about this rulemaking, is what happens to clinical judgment when a computer is making recommendations all day long. I've noticed it in myself and I've noticed it in younger nurses. When the alert system is mostly right, you start moving faster. You start trusting it. That's natural and honestly it's part of why these tools get adopted, because they do reduce some cognitive load in a busy shift. But it means that the times the AI is wrong are exactly the times nobody's paying the closest attention. The error that gets caught is the one that seems obviously wrong. The one that looks plausible — like Ruthie's — that's the one that slips through.⏎⏎I understand there are real benefits and I'm not saying don't move forward with any of this. Diagnostic tools that help catch things earlier, that flag drug interactions, that help with documentation — I've seen those help. But the pace of adoption HHS seems to be pushing toward, based on what I've read, worries me. Real-world performance validation on diverse patient populations should come before broad clinical deployment, not after. SYNTH-0096♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Kenneth Clark♔Howard University College of Medicine♔♔♔♔♔My concern here is not with AI adoption per se. I've spent the better part of two decades studying how health policy decisions made in high-confidence, resource-rich environments get deployed onto populations those environments never adequately represented, and what I keep seeing in this RFI is a familiar pattern: the acceleration language is in the foreground, and the equity infrastructure is either absent or deferred to future guidance.⏎⏎Let me be direct about what the evidence actually shows, because I think the Department may be understating how serious the replication problem already is. A 2024 survey through the American College of Surgeons Quality Programs network, covering 847 surgical departments across 42 states, found that 68% of respondents could not replicate vendor-reported AI performance metrics in their own patient populations. Not small deviations. Couldn't replicate them. And when we looked at the published validation literature for clinical AI in surgical settings, fewer than 23% of studies included prospective real-world outcome data outside the original development cohort. These findings were presented at the 2024 Clinical Congress. This isn't a theoretical gap I'm worried about. The gap is documented, it's substantial, and the RFI is proposing to put deployment incentives in front of it.⏎⏎The pulse oximetry literature is by now well established -- Sjoding et al. in NEJM, 2020, showed that pulse oximeters occult hypoxemia at significantly higher rates in Black patients, and subsequent work has shown this propagated into clinical decision support systems that were treating the oximeter reading as ground truth. The sepsis prediction algorithm issues in non-white populations are also in the peer-reviewed record. These aren't edge cases that got caught and corrected. They're examples of bias that persisted for years in deployed systems because aggregate performance metrics looked acceptable.⏎⏎What worries me about the framing of this RFI is that "accelerating adoption" in the absence of mandatory, standardized, pre-deployment bias auditing requirements will almost certainly reproduce this pattern at much larger scale. A system that performs well on aggregate accuracy measures can fail systematically for Black patients, for rural Medicaid beneficiaries, for patients with limited English proficiency -- and those failures will be invisible in the top-line numbers that vendors and health systems are likely to use to justify deployment. I've seen this in the surgical quality data. The populations who experience the worst failures are also the populations least likely to generate the kind of documented complaint that triggers a post-market review.⏎⏎I'm genuinely uncertain about what the right regulatory vehicle is here, whether this is primarily an FDA software-as-medical-device problem or an ONC certification problem or whether HHS needs to develop something new specifically for insurance and utilization management applications of AI, which are not obviously covered by either existing framework. That uncertainty is itself a problem, because vendors are not waiting for the framework to be resolved before deploying.⏎⏎The specific thing I'd ask HHS to address is this: what is the mechanism by which a hospital or health system that discovers a disparate impact from a deployed AI system is required to report that finding, to whom, on what timeline, and what happens to the vendor's authorization to continue operating that system in the meantime? I have not been able to find a clear answer to that question in the existing regulatory structure, and the RFI doesn't address it directly either.⏎⏎There's also the question of who bears the cost of prospective validation studies stratified by race, income, and geography. If that cost falls entirely on health systems or academic medical centers, the incentive to conduct that validation is low, particularly for smaller or safety-net institutions that are already operating under thin margins. The vendor community has a strong interest in submitting favorable pre-deployment data and a much weaker interest in funding the kind of ongoing post-deployment monitoring that would catch the failures I'm describing.⏎⏎I want to flag one more thing that I think doesn't get enough attention in these discussions. The automation bias literature is pretty consistent in showing that clinicians are less likely to override algorithmic recommendations than they are to override the same recommendation made by another clinician, even when the algorithmic recommendation is wrong. This means the harm from a biased AI system isn't just the bias in the output. It's the suppression of the clinical skepticism that might otherwise catch the error. And that suppression effect is probably strongest in high-volume, high-stress environments -- which is exactly where AI deployment is being prioritized because that's where efficiency gains look most attractive.⏎⏎I do think there are genuine potential benefits from clinical AI, particularly in imaging interpretation and early warning systems where the evidence base is stronger. I'm not arguing for a moratorium. But I keep coming back to the question of what HHS's theory of accountability is for the harms that will occur during an accelerated deployment period, and I haven't seen that articulated anywhere in this RFI with the specificity the question deserves.⏎⏎Kenneth Clark, PhD⏎Professor of Health Policy⏎Howard University College of Medicine⏎Washington, DC♔♔2026-01-15T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-15T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-24T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-22T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔AI systems in clinical settings inherit and amplify racial, socioeconomic, and institutional biases from training data, and their apparent authority makes those biases harder to detect and correct.♔academic♔medium♔frustrated♔Vermont♔professor of health policy♔57♔A 2024 survey conducted through the American College of Surgeons Quality Programs network, encompassing responses from 847 surgical departments across 42 states, found that 68% of respondents reported difficulty replicating vendor-reported AI performance metrics in their own patient populations. SYNTH-0099♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Richard Scott♔Data & Society Research Institute♔♔♔♔♔Data & Society Research Institute | HHS AI RFI | Docket No. HHS-2024-AI-CLINICAL⏎⏎Richard Scott, Research Director⏎Data & Society Research Institute⏎New York, NY⏎⏎Regarding Question Area: Governance and Accountability Mechanisms for AI in Clinical Settings⏎⏎Our member network — roughly 2,800 organizations, many of them community health centers and patient advocacy groups — started tracking AI-related patient complaints in earnest in early 2024. What we found was not subtle. Sixty-four percent of member organizations reported receiving patient complaints specifically tied to automated decision-making tools, and the complaints were not distributed evenly. They came disproportionately from facilities serving Medicaid-dependent populations and rural communities. I keep coming back to that number because I think it says something that the efficiency arguments don't.⏎⏎What patients were describing, across 14 focus groups we conducted at member sites, was not a technical failure. It was something more like being processed. They felt that a recommendation had been generated about their body by something they couldn't see, couldn't question, and didn't choose. And in most cases, they were right — fewer than one in three of our member organizations had any formal informed-consent protocol that specifically addressed AI-assisted clinical recommendations. Not because these organizations are careless, but because nobody required them to have one, and building one from scratch is genuinely hard when the underlying systems are opaque even to the clinicians using them.⏎⏎The thing that worries me about the framing of this RFI is the word "acceleration." I understand the impulse. There are real problems in clinical care that computational tools can help with — I'm not dismissing that. But medicine has always been partly about what happens in the room between a clinician and a patient. Not the data transfer, the actual human encounter. A patient who is scared and not entirely sure what's wrong with them communicates a lot of information that doesn't appear in a structured field anywhere. A family member who's been lying to the patient about the diagnosis. A person who's nodding along but clearly doesn't understand what they've been told. A man who says he's fine but whose body language says otherwise. These are not supplementary inputs that AI will eventually learn to process. They are the diagnostic encounter.⏎⏎The therapeutic relationship also has measurable clinical value — this is not sentimental, there's a real literature on it going back decades — and I think we should be asking whether acceleration risks degrading that relationship even when the AI recommendations themselves are technically sound. A technically correct suggestion that makes a patient feel like a data point is not a neutral outcome.⏎⏎The accountability question is the one where I'd like to see HHS do the most work, specifically. When a patient is harmed and an AI-assisted recommendation was part of the pathway to that harm, who is answerable? Right now the answer is murky in ways that I think most patients don't realize until something goes wrong. There's a structural difference between a physician who is genuinely responsible for a decision and a physician who is documenting compliance with an algorithmic output. Patients deserve to know which situation they're in.⏎⏎I'd specifically ask HHS to look at whether the informed-consent frameworks being developed around AI tools are actually reaching patients in plain language before decisions are made, not buried in intake paperwork. A member organization in upstate New York told us in Q2 2024 that their patients were signing forms acknowledging AI use without any staff member having explained what that meant in practice, because the staff themselves weren't sure.⏎⏎Data & Society Research Institute | HHS AI RFI | Page 2⏎⏎That gap — between the existence of a disclosure and a patient actually understanding what's being disclosed — is where a lot of the trust problem lives. And I don't think faster adoption solves it. SYNTH-0100♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Steven Martinez♔Change Healthcare♔♔♔♔♔My name is Steven Martinez. I'm a compliance officer at Change Healthcare, based in Oklahoma, and I've spent the better part of two years watching what happens downstream when payer-side AI systems make coverage decisions that nobody can adequately explain or contest. So when HHS frames this RFI primarily around accelerating AI adoption in clinical care, I find myself genuinely frustrated, because the acceleration problem is not where the harm is concentrated right now.⏎⏎The harm is in prior authorization. And I want to be specific about that.⏎⏎In our internal audit work across our technology portfolio, we've tracked how AI-driven PA systems deployed by major commercial payers are generating denial volumes that simply cannot be reconciled with meaningful human review at the point of decision. I'm not speculating about this. We see the adjudication data. We see the turnaround timestamps. A denial that would require a clinician to review imaging notes, chart history, and applicable coverage criteria in any responsible process is being resolved in under 30 seconds. That's not a determination. That's a classification. And the distinction matters enormously when the patient on the other end is a 72-year-old Medicare Advantage enrollee who doesn't know she has the right to appeal, and whose physician has already given up on the appeal process because the last three took 47 days to resolve and came back denied anyway.⏎⏎What I want HHS to address in this RFI — and I recognize this may not be the question you were most hoping to hear — is whether the Department intends to treat payer-side AI as part of the adoption problem it's trying to solve, or whether clinical-facing AI is the only category under consideration. Because if it's the latter, you are going to accelerate a two-tier system where AI tools at the point of care become more capable and more widely deployed, while the administrative AI sitting between the patient and access to that care remains completely unaccountable.⏎⏎The explainability requirements that get discussed in the context of diagnostic AI, the ones I actually think are reasonable and workable, should apply with at least equal force to automated denial systems. A denial is a clinical recommendation. It is a statement that a particular course of care is not medically necessary. If an AI system is making that statement, the patient and the treating physician are entitled to know what inputs drove that output. Not a boilerplate letter that cites a coverage policy. The actual logic. And there needs to be a human being with clinical credentials who is accountable for that decision before it goes out the door.⏎⏎I understand there's a position that this is really a CMS enforcement question, or a matter for state insurance regulators, and not something HHS can address in an AI adoption framework. I don't fully buy that. If HHS is going to use this process to shape how AI gets legitimized and reimbursed across the health sector, the scope of that conversation has to include the administrative layer. The 82% of health system partners in our advisory council who cited payer-to-payer certification inconsistency as a primary barrier to AI adoption are not talking about clinical diagnostic tools. They're talking about the fact that a product deployment can be approved for coverage under one payer's AI review criteria and denied under another's, with no consistent federal standard governing how that determination gets made or challenged.⏎⏎There's also a liability question that I don't think this RFI adequately frames. Four of the nine non-medical-device AI tools in our portfolio currently operate under ambiguous liability classifications. If a downstream clinical recommendation error occurs and the tool that contributed to it doesn't fall cleanly under FDA device classification, who is accountable? I genuinely don't know the answer to that, and I've been trying to get a clear answer for the better part of a year. The fact that I can't get one is itself a problem that HHS has the authority to help resolve, and I'd encourage the Department to be explicit about whether that's in scope here.⏎⏎One more thing. The populations most exposed to harm from unaccountable automated denials — elderly patients, disabled patients, Medicaid beneficiaries, non-English speakers — are also the populations least equipped to navigate an appeal process that assumes a level of health literacy and administrative persistence that most people don't have. I think about a specific case we saw reflected in claims data from a hospital system in Tulsa, Oklahoma around November of last year, where a prior auth denial for post-acute rehab following a stroke came back in under two minutes, and the patient was discharged home without the services her physician had ordered. Whether that denial was generated by an AI system I can't confirm definitively. But the timestamp is not consistent with human review, and the coverage rationale in the denial letter was a generic reference to a clinical guidelines document that had been superseded eight months earlier.⏎⏎That's the kind of thing I'd like HHS to think about before♔♔2026-02-08T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-08T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-24T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-22T00:00:00Z♔2♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔AI-driven insurance decisions and prior authorization systems automate the denial of care in ways that are opaque, difficult to appeal, and disproportionately harmful to vulnerable patients.♔industry♔high♔angry♔Oklahoma♔compliance officer♔35♔Echo IQ's internal compliance analysis of the 2023–2024 payer landscape identified that 68% of our AI-enabled diagnostic tools faced inconsistent coverage determinations across the 14 major commercial payers with whom our customers contract, resulting in an average adjudication delay of 47 days per new product deployment.
